From homotopy links to stratified homotopy theories
Sylvain Douteau, Lukas Waas

Abstract
. In previous work, the first author defined homotopy theories for stratified spaces from a simplicial and a topological perspective. In both frameworks stratified weak-equivalences are detected by suitable generalizations of homotopy links. These two frameworks are connected through a stratified version of the classical adjunction between the realization and the functor of singular simplices. Using a modified version of this adjunction, the first author showed that over a fixed poset of strata the two homotopy theories were equivalent. Building on this result we now show that the unmodified adjunction induces an equivalence between the global homotopy theories of stratified spaces and of stratified simplicial sets. We do so through an in depth study of the homotopy links. As a consequence, we prove that the classical homotopy theory of conically stratified spaces embeds fully-faithfully into the homotopy theory of all stratified spaces.



1.
Introduction

Stratifications and stratified spaces were first introduced by Whitney
[
Whi65
]
, Thom
[
Tho69
]
and Mather to describe manifolds with singularities. In this context, stratifications correspond to decompositions into strata that are themselves manifolds and satisfy certain compatibility conditions. Stratified spaces of this kind are usually called pseudo-manifolds, since they provide an extension of the class of manifolds over which many invariants can be extended while retaining their key properties. Most notably, intersection cohomology, introduced by Goresky and MacPherson in
[
GM80
]
is an extension of singular cohomology to pseudo-manifolds that still satisfies Poincaré Duality. Intersection cohomology is only an invariant up to stratum preserving homotopy equivalence, not arbitrary homotopy equivalences, which motivates the developpment of a homotopy theory for stratified spaces. This question was already asked by Goresky and MacPherson (see for example
[
B+08
, Problems 4 and 11]
).

Motivated by the study of the homotopical properties of stratified objects, Quinn
[
Qui88
]
introduced a more general notion of stratified spaces: Homotopically stratified sets. While for pseudo-manifolds compatibility conditions between the strata are characterized by geometric links, homotopically stratified sets make use of homotopy links (or holinks), which are spaces of paths going from one stratum to another.
As Miller later showed in
[
Mil13
, Theorem 6.3]
, stratified homotopy equivalences between homotopically stratified sets can be fully characterized as those maps inducing weak equivalences on all strata and homotopy links. This inspired the idea that homotopy links and strata should be the basic blocks for defining a stratified homotopy type.

Let us now recall a few classical results of homotopy theory: In
[
Qui67
]
Quillen introduced the notion of a model category and immediately gave two seminal examples. The model category of topological spaces,
\mathrm{Top}
, and the model category of simplicial sets,
\mathrm{sSet}
. He also showed that the adjunction relating those two categories
{}|-|\colon\mathrm{sSet}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Top}\colon\mathrm{Sing}
was in fact a Quillen equivalence, meaning that the two homotopy theories are equivalent. This is a key result in homotopy theory since it allows one to prove results about the homotopy theory of spaces while working in a purely combinatorial setting.

In this paper, we consider stratified spaces in the broadest sense - those are just spaces with a continuous map towards a poset,
X\to P
, as defined by Woolf in
[
Woo09
]
and popularized by Lurie in
[
Lur17
]
. This point of view gives rise to two kind of categories. First, the categories of stratified objects and stratum preserving map over a fixed poset. Here, objects can be taken to mean either topological spaces or simplicial sets, producing the categories
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
(resp
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
), of topological spaces (resp simplicial sets) stratified over the poset
P
. Secondly, there are the categories of stratified objects over all posets, where maps are given by commutative squares. Those are denoted
\mathrm{Strat}
and
s\mathrm{Strat}
for the topological and simplicial versions. Note that the categories
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
, correspond to the fibers of the functor
\mathrm{Strat}\to\text{Poset}
over the discrete categories
\{P\}
, and similarly for
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
and
s\mathrm{Strat}
.

The emerging field of stratified homotopy theory has seen a lot of recent activity (See for example
[
AFR19
,
NL19
,
Hai18
]
, and
[
Lur17
, Appendix A]
) with applications to algebraic geometry
[
BGH18
]
,
G
-isovariant homotopy theory
[
KY21
]
, and to the study of classical invariants of stratified spaces
[
CT20
]
.
In this context, the first author showed that there exist two independent model structures on the category
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
, of spaces
[
Dou21b
]
and
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, of simplicial sets
[
Dou21c
]
, stratified over a fixed poset
P
. See also
[
Dou19
]
. Both are defined from appropriate notions of homotopy links (and generalization of those to tuples of strata), i.e. weak-equivalences between stratified objects are maps inducing weak-equivalences between all strata and homotopy links. In addition, it is shown in
[
Dou21b
]
that the model structures on
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
and
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, for varying
P
, assemble to form model structures on
\mathrm{Strat}
and
s\mathrm{Strat}
respectively. Furthermore, the classical adjunction
{}|-|\colon\mathrm{sSet}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Top}\colon\mathrm{Sing}
admits stratified versions,
{}{}|-|_{P}\colon\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Top}_{P}\colon\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
, for all posets
P
. Those can be glued together, producing a global adjunction
{}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}}\colon s\mathrm{Strat}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Strat}\colon\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}
.

The adjunction
{}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}}\colon s\mathrm{Strat}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Strat}\colon\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}
is not a Quillen equivalence, however. In fact, if
P
is not discrete, the adjunction
{}{}|-|_{P}\colon\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Top}_{P}\colon\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
is not even a Quillen adjunction. Nevertheless, by composing the above adjunction with a suitably defined stratified subdivision, the first author showed in
[
Dou21a
]
that there exists a modified Quillen equivalence

(1)

{}{}|\mathrm{sd}_{P}(-)|_{P}\colon\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Top}_{P}\colon\mathrm{Ex}_{P}\mathrm{Sing}_{P},

meaning that the homotopy theory of spaces, and simplicial sets, stratified over the same poset, coincide.
On the other hand the adjunction (
1
) is no longer compatible with the gluing process producing
\mathrm{Strat}
and
s\mathrm{Strat}
, which means that it does not allow for a direct comparison between the homotopy theory associated to the two global model categories.

Relatedly, one would want to interpret Miller’s theorem
[
Mil13
, Theorem 6.3]
- which characterizes stratum-preseving homotopy equivalences between suitably nice stratified spaces - as a statement about cofibrant-fibrant objects in a model category. But in fact, the objects appearing in Miller’s theorem are almost never cofibrant as objects of
\mathrm{Strat}
.

Both of those observations might motivate one to consider some other model structure for the category of stratified spaces, to remedy these problems. It turns out however that such a structure does not exist, without making major changes to the topological setup, as we show in
Appendix

A
. What we show instead is that even though the adjunctions
({}{}|-|_{P},\mathrm{Sing}_{P})
and
({}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}},\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}})
are not Quillen equivalences, they still induce - in a very strong sense - isomorphisms between the homotopy theories of stratified simplicial sets and stratified spaces (see
Theorem

5.1
and
Remark

5.4
).

Theorem 1.1
.

The adjoint pairs
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
and
{}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}
descend to equivalences of homotopy categories

\displaystyle{}{}|-|_{P}\colon\mathrm{Ho}\mathrm{sSet}_{P}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Ho}\mathrm{Top}_{P}\colon\mathrm{Sing}_{P},

\displaystyle{}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}}\colon\mathrm{Ho}s\mathrm{Strat}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Ho}\mathrm{Strat}\colon\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}.

Note that an even stronger statement holds. Instead of the homotopy categories, one can consider the simplicial localizations in the sense of Dwyer and Kan
[
DK80a
]
. Then, one sees that in addition to inducing equivalences between the homotopy categories, the pairs
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
and
{}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}
induce Dwyer-Kan equivalences between the simplicial localizations. In particular, this means that the
\infty
-categories associated to stratified spaces and stratified simplicial sets are equivalent.

We also consider the case of triangulable conically stratified spaces (see
Remark

2.28
). As mentioned earlier, stratum preserving homotopy equivalences between those spaces can be explicitly characterized (see
[
Mil13
, Theorem 6,3]
). In particular, for those objects weak-equivalences and stratum preserving homotopy equivalences coincide. In fact, even though those objects are not fibrant-cofibrant, they behave as if they were. What this means is that in order to study their homotopy theory, one does not need to work with their hom-set in the homotopy category
\mathrm{Ho}\mathrm{Strat}
, but one can work instead with the much more explicit set of stratified maps up to stratum preserving homotopies, because the two coincide. This can be phrased more rigorously as follows (
Corollaries

5.8
and
5.9
):

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\mathrm{Con}\subset\mathrm{Strat}
be the full subcategory of triangulable conically stratified spaces, and
\simeq
the relation of stratified homotopy. Then the induced functor

\mathrm{Con}/{\simeq}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ho}\mathrm{Strat}

is a fully faithful embedding.

Let
\mathrm{Con}_{P}\subset\mathrm{Top}_{P}
be the full subcategory of triangulable conically stratified spaces over
P
, and
\simeq_{P}
be the relation of stratum preserving homotopy. Then the induced functor

\mathrm{Con}_{P}/{\simeq_{P}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ho}\mathrm{Top}_{P}

is a fully faithful embedding.

Again, this statement can also be strengthened to a fully faithful inclusion of infinity categories.
In the proofs contained in this paper, we mainly focus on stratified objects over a fixed poset,
P
. In this context,
our approach is twofold. On one hand, we give a precise comparison of three model categories, and in particular of their classes of weak-equivalences. Those model categories are those of spaces and simplicial sets stratified over
P
, as well as the category of diagrams of simplicial sets, indexed by strictly increasing chains in
P
(which we call regular flags). An object in this last category corresponds to the data of the strata and (generalized) homotopy links of a stratified object. This is summed up in the following theorem (
Corollaries

4.22
,
4.23
,
4.24
,
3.16
and
6.2
).

Theorem 1.3
.

All functors in the following commutative diagram preserve and characterize weak-equivalences between arbitrary objects.

(2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sSet}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Diag}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Top}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|-|_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sing}_{P}}

Furthermore, all of these functors descend to equivalences of the underlying homotopy categories.

Again,
Theorem

1.3
strengthens to a statement about the corresponding infinity categories.

Theorem

1.3
suggests a somewhat richer picture than what one has in the non-stratified case. Indeed, in the case where
P=\{*\}
, the categories
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
and
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
are both canonically equivalent to
\mathrm{sSet}
, turning this triangle into the familiar adjoint pair
\mathrm{sSet}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Top}
. In general however, the categories
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
and
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
are very different, and the usefulness of thinking in terms of diagrams is illustrated in
Sections

6.2
and
B
, through the lens of vertical objects.

Furthermore, the fact that the functors in Diagram (
2
) preserve and reflect all weak-equivalences is a stronger property than one might expect. Indeed, four of those six functors are part of Quillen equivalences. One expects such a functor to only reflect weak-equivalences either between cofibrant or between fibrant objects. In fact, weak-equivalences in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
can even be defined as those maps that are sent to weak-equivalences in
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
,
after
a suitable fibrant replacement.
Theorem

1.3
then implies that this fibrant replacement is not needed. This gives some insight as to why the model structure on
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
described by Henriques in
[
Hen
]
and the one studied here coincide (see
Remark

6.3
).

On the other hand, we also have a more topological approach. Recall that pseudo-manifolds (and more generally conically stratified objects) can be described locally via the data of their strata and their (local) links. Indeed, in such a space, any stratum has a neighborhood that is locally homeomorphic to the product of the stratum and a cone on the (local) link. This is the phenomenon that we want to generalize to be able to reconstruct a stratified homotopy type, from the data of strata and homotopy links.

First note that the geometric definitions for the (global) link in terms of a boundary of a regular neighborhood readily extend to arbitrary stratified simplicial objects. This can be done either through the simplicial structure (by making use of the subdivision), or by working topologically. On the other hand, the homotopy links - which are nothing more than spaces of exit-paths - can be defined, as certain mapping spaces, for arbitrary stratified objects. Though,
a priori
, the result might depend on the category in which we compute those mapping spaces.

Note also that while the historical definition of homotopy links, given by Quinn in
[
Qui88
]
, is only concerned with pairs of strata
[p<q]
, we study a generalized version of those homotopy links, which is defined for any increasing chain of strata
\mathcal{I}=[p_{0}<\dots<p_{n}]
. In this case, instead of exit-paths, the elements of the
\mathcal{I}
-th homotopy link of a stratified object can be thought of as "exit-simplices".

We show that, for a stratified simplicial set, all definitions of links and homotopy links coincide up to weak-equivalence (see
Theorems

4.21
,
6.1
and
6.3
). This is summed up in the following theorem, where the geometric interpretations given for the different definitions hold when investigating pairs of strata, i.e. when
\mathcal{I}=[p<q]
. The two notions of links are illustrated in
Fig.

1
and elements of the three different homotopy links are represented in
Fig.

2
.

Figure 1.

A simplicial set
K
stratified over
P=\{0<1\}
, its simplicial link,
\mathrm{Link}_{[0<1]}(K)
, and the topological link,
{}|K|_{b}
, with
b
in the interior of the interval
N(\{0<1\})
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
be a stratified simplicial set,
\mathcal{I}
a flag and
b\in{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
the barycenter. Then the following spaces are weakly equivalent:

•

{}|\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)|
, a simplicial version of the space of exit-paths,

•

{}|\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(K^{\mathrm{fib}})|
, same as the above, but computed on a fibrant replacement,

•

{}|\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)|
, the usual simplicial link, defined in terms of the subdivision,

•

{}|K|_{b}
, a geometric notion of link, defined from the realization,

•

\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})
, a space of exit-paths with extra conditions,

•

\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})
, the space of exit-paths defined by Quinn.

Figure 2.
In green, from left to right, a
0
-simplex in
\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})
, an exit path in
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{N(P)})
and an exit-path in
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P})
, with
P=\{0<1\}
,
\mathcal{I}=[0<1]
, and
\mathcal{J}=[0\leq 1\leq 1]

While
Theorems

1.3
and
1.4
might look very different, they are closely related. Indeed, in the model categories we study, weak-equivalences are defined in terms of strata and homotopy links, in the sense that a map is a weak-equivalence if and only if it induces weak-equivalences between all strata and homotopy links. This means that a comparison between different notions of homotopy links immediatly induces a comparison between the corresponding class of weak-equivalences. The converse is also true, albeit in a less straightforward way. In fact, we prove
Theorems

1.3
and
1.4
in parallel throughout the paper, going back and forth between the topological and the homotopical point of view.

The article is organized as follows.

Section

2
contains a recollection of all the necessary notions from
[
Dou21c
,
Dou21b
,
Dou21a
]
, as well as definitions for all notions of link and homotopy link appearing in this article.

In
Section

3
, we prove that
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
characterizes all weak-equivalences. We do so by first proving in
Section

3.1
that the natural map
K\to\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(K)
is a strong anodyne extension. In particular, this completes the proof that
\mathrm{Ex}^{\infty}_{P}
is a fibrant replacement functor for stratified simplicial sets, see
Corollary

3.2
. The proof relies on the notion of strong anodyne extensions, introduced by Moss in
[
Mos19
]
and already applied to stratified simplicial sets in
[
Dou21c
]
.

In
Section

4
we prove that the functor
{}{}|-|_{P}\colon\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\to\mathrm{Top}_{P}
characterizes weak-equivalences between arbitrary objects. We do so by showing that, for a stratified simplicial set
K
,
{}{}|\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)|_{P}
,
{}|K|_{b}
,
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})
and
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})
are all weakly-equivalent. The key technical part, which consists mostly of point-set topology, is the proof that
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})
and
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})
are weakly equivalent.

In
Section

5
, we use the results of
Sections

3
and
4
to show that the adjunctions
{}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}
and
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
descend to equivalences between the homotopy categories of stratified simplicial sets and stratified spaces.
We then use this result to show that the homotopy theory of triangulable conically stratified objects embeds fully faithfully in the homotopy category of stratified spaces (
Corollary

5.8
). Finally, in
Section

5.3
, we deduce a stratified simplicial approximation theorem from the comparison between the homotopy categories.

In
Section

6
, we prove that for a stratified simplicial set
K
, the holinks
{}|\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)|
,
{}|\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(K^{\mathrm{fib}})|
and
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})
are all weakly-equivalent. We do so by investigating a well-behaved classes of stratified spaces, vertically stratified CW-complexes and simplicial sets, in
Section

6.2
and proving a series of approximation theorems (
Propositions

6.17
and
6.24
). Along the way, we show that the class of vertically stratified CW-complex, with their vertical maps and homotopies, model the homotopy category of stratified spaces (see
Theorem

6.26
). Finally,
Section

6.3
gives the proof of
Proposition

6.7
which is the key technical argument in comparing the homotopy links.

In
Appendix

A
, we give an example of a particular stratum preserving map which obstructs the transport of the model structure from
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
to
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
, and constrains the kind of model structures that one can hope to obtain on
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
. We also discuss how this example translates for other work on the subject such as
[
NL19
]
and
[
Hai18
]
.

Appendix

B
expands on
Section

6.2
, making the relationship between vertical objects and diagrams more precise, and giving an alternate higher level proof of
Theorem

6.26
.

2.
Preliminaries

We begin by recalling the framework of stratified homotopy theory used in
[
Dou21c
,
Dou21b
,
Dou21a
]
as well as several of the central results of the theory.
These preliminaries are intended to be rather exhaustive, making the remainder of the paper generally accessible to the reader with general knowledge of model categories and abstract homotopy theory. While introducing the necessary language and notation, these preliminaries also serve to put the results discussed in
Section

1
into rigorous context. The reader already familiar with the framework used in
[
Dou21c
,
Dou21b
,
Dou21a
]
can safely skip most of those preliminaries, reading only
Section

2.5
for the definitions of generalized links and homotopy links and
Sections

2.10
,
2.11
and
2.12
for a precise recollection of the needed results.

First, we introduce the categories of stratified objects we are studying (
Sections

2.1
,
2.2
and
2.3
). We equip these objects with notions of generalized homotopy links and introduce the basic relationships between the latter (
Sections

2.4
,
2.5
and
2.6
). These generalized homotopy links entail notions of weak equivalence on categories of stratified objects in question, which are integrated into model structures in
Sections

2.7
,
2.8
and
2.9
. Finally, the homotopical properties of the functors connecting the resulting model categories are discussed in
Sections

2.10
,
2.11
and
2.12
.

2.1.
Partially ordered sets

We begin by recalling a few general construction for partially ordered sets, which will serve as the indexing sets for stratifications.

Definition 2.1
.

Recall that a partially ordered set (or poset) is the data of a set
P
, equipped with an irreflexive, transitive and asymmetric relation, that is generically denoted
"{<}"
. We will also consider the weak relation,
"{\leq}"
, defined in the usual way via
p\leq q
if
p<q
or
p=q
. In this paper, a poset map is a map
\alpha\colon P\to Q
such that if
p\leq p^{\prime}
in
P
, then
\alpha(p)\leq\alpha(p^{\prime})
in
Q
. The category of posets and poset maps is denoted
\mathrm{Poset}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Given a poset,
P
, define the order topology on
P
as follows. A subset
A\subset P
is closed if and only if it is a downset, i.e. it fulfills the condition:

p\in A,q\leq p\Rightarrow q\in A.

Remark 2.3
.

A basis of closed sets for this topology is given by the sets
A_{p}
,
p\in P
, defined as follows:

A_{p}=\{q\in P\mid q\leq p\}.

Remark 2.4
.

Note that given a map of sets
\alpha\colon P\to Q
between posets, the map
\alpha
is a map of posets if and only if it is a continuous map between the posets equipped with their order topologies. This means that the order topology gives a fully-faithful functor
\mathrm{Poset}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Top}
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
P
be a poset.

•

A
flag
of
P
,
\mathcal{J}
, is a finite sequence in
P
,
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
.

•

A
regular flag
of
P
,
\mathcal{I}
, is a finite sequence in
P
with no repeated entries
\mathcal{I}=[q_{0}<\dots<q_{k}]
. We will usually reserve the letter
\mathcal{I}
for regular flags.

•

Given a flag
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
, we denote its underlying regular flag (obtained by deleting repeated entries) by
\{p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}\}
.

A map of flags
f\colon[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]\to[q_{0}\leq\dots\leq q_{m}]
is a non decreasing map
f\colon\{0,\dots,n\}\to\{0,\dots,m\}
such that
q_{f(i)}=p_{i}
, for all
0\leq i\leq n
. Let
\Delta(P)
be the category of flags and maps of flags and
R(P)\subset\Delta(P)
be the full subcategory of regular flags.

Definition 2.6
.

The
nerve of a poset

P
is the simplicial set
N(P)
whose simplices are the flags of
P
. The
k
-th face operation is given by omitting the
k
-th entry in a flag while the
k
-th degeneracy is given by repeating the
k
-th entry. For
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
, we will write
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
for the associated
n
-simplex of
N(P)
,
\Delta^{n}\to N(P)
, seen as a simplicial set.

Remark 2.7
.

Note that, as was the case for spaces, a map of sets
\alpha\colon P\to Q
between posets is a map of posets if and only if it extends to a simplicial map
N(\alpha)\colon N(P)\to N(Q)
. In other words, there is a fully-faithful embedding
N\colon\mathrm{Poset}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{sSet}
.

Remark 2.8
.

It is also classical to see a poset as a (small) category where there are no non-identity endomorphisms, and where there is at most one map between two objects. Starting from a set-theoretic poset
P
one gets such a category by taking
P
to be the set of objects, and having an arrow from
p\to q
whenever
p\leq q
. Note that from this point of view, the nerve of the poset is nothing more than the nerve of the corresponding category. Similarly, the category of regular flags
R(P)
is the subdivision of
P
, seen as a category.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
P
be a poset. Define
\varphi_{P}\colon{}|N(P)|\to P
as follows. For
\mathcal{I}=[p_{0}<\dots<p_{n}]
a regular flag, using the identification

{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|\cong\{(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})\mid 0\leq t_{i}\leq 1,\ 0\leq i\leq n,\ \sum_{i}t_{i}=1\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

we set

\varphi_{P}(t_{0},\dots,t_{n})=p_{m},\ m=\max\{i\mid t_{i}\not=0\}.

2.2.
Stratified spaces

Before we define stratified spaces, a technical remark about the nature of the topological spaces considered is in order.

Remark 2.10
.

In this paper,
\mathrm{Top}
stands for the category of
\Delta
-generated spaces (see
[
Dug03
]
and
[
FR08
]
) and all continuous maps between them. This is to ensure that the category is locally presentable, which is needed to get the model structure described in
Theorem

2.43
(see also
Remark

2.47
). It shouldn’t be of much concern however, since all the usual examples of stratified spaces, as well as posets themselves, are
\Delta
-generated spaces. The implications of this choice of category for mapping spaces are discussed in
Remark

2.14
.

In this paper, we consider the most general notion of stratified spaces among those commonly used.

Definition 2.11
.

A
stratified space
is the data of:

•

a topological space
X
,

•

a poset
P
,

•

a continuous map
\varphi_{X}\colon X\to P
, called the
stratification
.

By abuse of notation we will often refer to the above data just by the space
X
.
The
strata
of
X
are the subspaces
\varphi_{X}^{-1}(p)=X^{p}\subset X
for
p\in P
. A
stratified map
between two stratified spaces
X\to P
and
Y\to Q
is a commutative square of continuous maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}}

We will often refer to such a square just by the map
f
.
The category of stratified spaces and stratified maps is denoted
\mathrm{Strat}
.

Given a poset
P
, a
stratum preserving
map between two spaces stratified over
P
,
X
and
Y
, is a continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
such that the following triangle commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{Y}}

The category of spaces stratified over
P
and stratum preserving maps is denoted
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
.

Remark 2.12
.

Consider the functor
\mathrm{Strat}\to\mathrm{Poset}
sending
X\to P
to
P
and a stratified map
f
to
\bar{f}
. This functor is a bifibration (see
[
CM20
, Section 2.1]
), and the fiber over a poset
P
is the subcategory
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
. This situation is pictured in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Top}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Strat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{P,1_{P}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Poset}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,.}}

Recollection 2.13
.

The category
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
admits the structure of a simplicial category. The tensoring of
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
for
X\in\mathrm{Top}_{P}
and
S\in\mathrm{sSet}
is given by
X\times{}|S|
with the stratification induced by first projecting to the first component. By abuse of notation, this stratified space will be denoted
X\times S
. Consequently, for
X,Y\in\mathrm{Top}_{P}
the simplicial hom is defined via

\mathrm{Map}(X,Y)_{n}=\mathrm{Top}_{P}(X\times\Delta^{n},Y).

Furthermore,
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
is also a tensored and cotensored category over
\mathrm{Top}
, again with the tensoring induced by taking the product and stratifying via projection to the stratified component. The mapping space

\mathcal{C}^{0}_{P}(X,Y)\subset\mathcal{C}^{0}(X,Y)

is obtained by considering the topology on
\mathrm{Top}_{P}(X,Y)
induced by the inclusion into
\mathcal{C}^{0}(X,Y)
.
By construction, we obtain natural isomorphisms

\mathrm{Sing}\big(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{P}(X,Y)\big)\cong\mathrm{Map}(X,Y)

relating the simplicial and the topological enrichment of
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
.

Note that the category
\mathrm{Strat}
also admits simplicial and topological enrichment, defined in a similar way, as well as internal hom-sets (see
[
NL19
, section 6.2]
).

Remark 2.14
.

There is some ambiguity on the topology that should be assigned to the mapping space
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{P}(X,Y)
. In this work, the category
\mathrm{Top}
stands for the category of
\Delta
-generated space, (see
[
FR08
,
Dug03
]
), and so
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{P}(X,Y)
should be equipped with the
\Delta
-ification of the compact open topology. On the other hand, note that
\mathrm{Sing}(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{P}(X,Y))
gives the same simplicial set whether one uses the compact open topology or its
\Delta
-ification, and in particular, the map
\colon\mathcal{C}^{0,\Delta}_{P}(X,Y)\to\mathcal{C}^{0,\text{c.o.}}_{P}(X,Y)
given by the change in topology is a weak equivalence. Since we are only interested in the (weak) homotopy type of those objects, we will always consider
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{P}(X,Y)
as equipped with the compact open topology.

Furthermore, if
X
is some compact stratified space and
K
is a locally finite stratified simplicial set, then
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{P}(X,{}{}|K|_{P})
will be metrizable. In particular,
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})=\mathcal{C}^{0}_{P}({}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P},{}{}|K|_{P})
, will be metrizable if
K
is a locally finite stratified simplicial set (see
Definition

2.31
). This observation will be usefull in the proof of
Theorem

4.8
.

The simplicial structure immediatly induces a notion of homotopy.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
f,g\colon X\to Y
be two maps in
\mathrm{Strat}
. A
stratified homotopy
between
f
and
g
is the data of a stratified map
H\colon X\times\Delta^{1}\to Y
such that the restriction to
X\times\{0\}
and
X\times\{1\}
are equal to
f
and
g
respectively. We denote this relation by
f\simeq_{\mathrm{Strat}}g
.
A
stratified homotopy equivalence
is a stratified map
f\colon X\to Y
such that there exists a stratified map
g\colon Y\to X
, such that
g\circ f\simeq_{\mathrm{Strat}}1_{X}
and
f\circ g\simeq_{\mathrm{Strat}}1_{Y}
. For
X,Y\in\mathrm{Top}_{P}
, we will write
[X,Y]_{P}
for the
set of stratified homotopy classes of stratum preserving maps between
X
and
Y
.

Remark 2.16
.

One can rephrase the above definition as follows: given two stratified spaces
X\to P
and
Y\to Q
and two maps between them,
f
and
g
, a stratified homotopy between
f
and
g
corresponds to a commutative square in
\mathrm{Top}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times[0,1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{X}\circ pr_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{H}}

There are several things to note here.

•

If two maps are stratified homotopic, the underlying maps of spaces are homotopic, since
H
is also a homotopy in the usual sense.

•

Since the homotopy is constant at the level of posets, one must have
\bar{H}=\bar{f}=\bar{g}
. In particular, any stratified homotopy between stratum preserving maps is given by a stratum preserving homotopy,
H
. For this reason we will not make a distinction between stratum-preserving and stratified homotopies, and just use "stratified homotopy" as a generic term.

•

Since the homotopy is constant at the poset level, any stratified homotopy equivalence must be over an isomorphism of posets.

We will also make use of a stronger notion of stratified spaces, for which, in addition to a decomposition into strata, the stratification also encodes information about the neighborhoods of strata.

Definition 2.17
.

A
strongly stratified space
is the data of

•

a space
X
,

•

a poset
P
,

•

a continuous map
\varphi_{X}\colon X\to{}|N(P)|
called the
(strong) stratification
.

Stratum-preserving maps
between strongly stratified spaces are defined analogously to
Definition

2.11
as commutative triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|N(P)|}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{Y}}

This leads to the category
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
, of spaces strongly stratified over
P
and stratum preserving maps. By abuse of language, we will call the objects of
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
spaces stratified over
N(P)
.

Recollection 2.18
.

The map
\varphi_{P}\colon{}|N(P)|\to P
, of
Definition

2.9
induces a functor:

\displaystyle\varphi_{P}\circ-\colon\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Top}_{P}

\displaystyle(X,\varphi_{X}\colon X\to{}|N(P)|)

\displaystyle\mapsto(X,\varphi_{P}\circ\varphi_{X}\colon X\to P).

This functor admits a right-adjoint,
-\times_{P}{}|N(P)|\colon\mathrm{Top}_{P}\to\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
, defined on objects as the following pull-back:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times_{P}{}|N(P)|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|N(P)|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{P}}

where the strong stratification on
Y\times_{P}{}|N(P)|
is given by the projection on the second factor.
Note that
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
also admits a simplicial and topological enrichment, as in
2.13
, which analogously to the stratified setting, induces a notion of
strongly stratified homotopies
. These enrichment structures are compatible with the adjunction
\varphi_{P}\circ-\dashv-\times_{P}{}|N(P)|
. In particular, this adjoint pair preserves the respective notions of stratified homotopies.

2.3.
Stratified simplicial sets

We will also consider a simplicial version of stratified spaces, the stratified simplicial sets.

Definition 2.19
.

A
stratified simplicial set
is the data of:

•

a simplicial set
K
,

•

a poset
P
,

•

a simplicial map
\varphi_{K}\colon K\to N(P)
, called the
stratification
.

By abuse of notation, we will often refer to the above data just by the simplicial set
K
. A
stratified map
between two stratified simplicial sets
K\to N(P)
,
L\to N(Q)
is a commutative square of simplicial maps.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(Q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(\bar{f})}

The category of stratified simplicial sets and stratified maps is denoted
s\mathrm{Strat}
.

Given a poset
P
, a
stratum preserving map
between two simplicial sets stratified over
P
,
K
and
L
, is a simplicial map
f\colon K\to L
, such that the following triangle commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(P)\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{L}}

The category of simplicial sets stratified over
P
is denoted
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
.

Remark 2.20
.

The category
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
can alternatively be defined as a presheaf category. Indeed, there is an equivalence of categories
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\cong\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta(P)^{op},\text{Set})
(see
Definition

2.5
). Note that under this identification, a flag
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
is sent to the stratified simplicial set
\varphi_{\mathcal{J}}\colon\Delta^{n}\to N(P)
, satisfying
\varphi_{\mathcal{J}}(k)=p_{k}
, where
k
stands for the
k
-th vertex of
\Delta^{n}
. We will denote this stratified simplicial set
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
, and call all such objects stratified simplices. See
[
Dou21c
, Proposition 1.3]
. Note that this is consistent with the convention of
Definition

2.6
.

Recollection 2.21
.

The category
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
admits the structure of a simplicial category. The tensoring between
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
and
S\in\mathrm{sSet}
is given by the product
K\times S
with the stratification given by the composition
K\times S\to K\to N(P)
. For
K,L\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
the simplicial hom is defined via

\mathrm{Map}(K,L)_{n}=\mathrm{sSet}_{P}(K\times\Delta^{n},L).

Just as for stratified spaces, the simplicial structure induces a notion of homotopy equivalences.

Definition 2.22
.

Let
f,g\colon K\to L
be two maps in
s\mathrm{Strat}
. An
elementary stratified homotopy
between
f
and
g
is the data of a stratified map
H\colon K\times\Delta^{1}\to L
, whose restrictions to
K\times\{0\}
and
K\times\{1\}
give
f
and
g
respectively. The maps
f
and
g
are said to be
stratified homotopic
if there exists a finite sequence of maps
f_{i}
,
0\leq i\leq n
such that
f_{0}=f
,
f_{n}=g
, and for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
,
f_{i}
and
f_{i+1}
are related by an elementary stratified homotopy. A
stratified homotopy equivalence
is a stratified map
f\colon K\to L
such that there exist a stratified map
g\colon L\to K
such that
g\circ f
and
f\circ g
are respectively stratified homotopic to
1_{K}
and
1_{L}
. For
K,L\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, we will write
[K,L]_{P}
for the set of stratum preserving maps between
K
and
L
up to stratified homotopy.

Recollection 2.23
.

The categories of stratified simplicial sets and stratified spaces are connected through a
{}|-|\dashv\mathrm{Sing}
style adjunction, just as in the unstratified context. It can be described explicitely as follows. Given some fixed poset
P
, we define a realization style functor

\displaystyle{}|-|_{N(P)}\colon\mathrm{sSet}_{P}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}

\displaystyle\left(\varphi_{K}\colon K\to N(P)\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left({}|\varphi_{K}|\colon{}|K|\to{}|N(P)|\right).

Composing with the functor
\varphi_{P}\circ-\colon\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}\to\mathrm{Top}_{P}
(see
2.18
) gives a functor
{}{}|-|_{P}\colon\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\to\mathrm{Top}_{P}
. Both functors admit right adjoints,
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}\colon\mathrm{Top}_{P}\to\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
and
\mathrm{Sing}_{N(P)}\colon\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}\to\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, defined as follows. Given a stratified space
\varphi_{X}\colon X\to P
, its
stratified singular simplicial set
,
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)
, is given by the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sing}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sing}(P)\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sing}(\varphi_{X})}

where the bottom map is the adjoint map to
\varphi_{P}
from
Definition

2.9
under
{}|-|\dashv\mathrm{Sing}
.
Equivalently, as a presheaf on
\Delta(P)
,
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)
can be constructed via

\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)_{\mathcal{J}}=\mathrm{Top}_{P}({}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P},X).

Note that the collection of adjunctions
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
extend to an adjunction

{}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}}\colon\mathrm{sStrat}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Strat}\colon\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}.

The definition of
\mathrm{Sing}_{N(P)}
is entirely analogous.

Note also that the adjunctions
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
and
{}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}
are compatible with the simplicial structures (see
[
Dou21c
, Proposition 4.9]
).

Proposition 2.24
.

The adjunctions
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
and
{}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}
are simplicial, and preserve stratified homotopies (see
Definitions

2.15
and
2.22
).

2.4.
Classical links and homotopy links

Recall that pseudo-manifolds are particular kinds of stratified spaces whose strata are manifolds satisfying gluing conditions. Those conditions can be expressed through the use of some smooth structures, as in
[
Whi65
]
or
[
Tho69
]
. Or they can be expressed in a purely topological fashion by asking that all points should have neighborhood homeomorphic to cones. We recall the more recent and more general definition of
conically stratified spaces
, which will be sufficient for our purpose. Note that pseudo-manifolds are examples of conically stratified spaces.

Definition 2.25

(
[
Lur17
, Definition A.5.5]
)
.

Let
P
be a poset, and define
c(P)=\{*\}\coprod P
, with
*<p
for all
p\in P
. Let
\varphi_{L}\colon L\to P
be a stratified space. Its (stratified)
cone
is the stratified space

\displaystyle c(L)=L\times[0,1)/L\times\{0\}

\displaystyle\to c(P)

\displaystyle(l,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\varphi_{L}(l)&\text{, if $t>0$}\\
&\text{, if $t=0$.}\end{array}\right.

equipped with the teardrop topology, (see
Remark

2.26
).
A stratified space
X\to P
is
conically stratified
if, for every
x\in X
, in some stratum
X^{p}
, there exists

•

a stratified space
L\to P_{>p}=\{q\in P\mid q>p\}
, the
(local) link at
x
,

•

an open neighborhood
x\in U\subset X
,

•

a space
Z
,

•

and a stratified homeomorphism
Z\times c(L)\cong U
, over the poset identification
c(P_{>p})\cong P_{\geq p}\subset P
.

Remark 2.26
.

In the above definition, one considers the teardrop topology on the cone
c(L)
(see
[
Lur17
, Definition A.5.3]
). Note that for a compact (stratified) space,
L
, it coincides with the quotient topology. Since pseudo-manifolds are always assumed to have compact links, this subtlety does not come into play when studying those objects. However, when studying more general examples of conically stratified spaces, this distinction is crucial. In fact, we will use throughout a result of Lurie
[
Lur17
, Theorem A.6.4]
(see
Theorem

2.59
) whose proof relies on the properties of the teardrop topology.

An even more general notion, more suited for the study of stratified homotopies, was introduced by Quinn in
[
Qui88
]
, that of homotopically stratified sets. For those objects, instead of considering local links, one considers pairwise homotopy links, which provide a homotopy theoretic global approach to the former.

Definition 2.27

(
[
Qui88
, Definition 2.1]
)
.

Let
X\to P
be a stratified space and
p<q\in P
. The
homotopy link
of the
p
-stratum in the
q
-stratum is the topological space

\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{p<q}(X)=\{\gamma\colon[0,1]\to X\mid\gamma(0)\in X^{p},\ \gamma(t)\in X^{q},\ \forall t>0\},

whose topology is induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{p<q}(X)\subset C^{0}([0,1],X)
.

Quinn then defined (see
[
Qui88
, Definition 3.1]
)
homotopically stratified sets
as those (metric) stratified spaces satisfying:

•

evaluation at
0
,
\mathrm{ev}_{0}\colon\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{p<q}(X)\to X^{p}
is a fibration in
\mathrm{Top}
, for all
p<q\in P
,

•

inclusions
X^{p}\hookrightarrow X^{p}\cup X^{q}
are tame, for all
p<q\in P
.

Remark 2.28
.

In this paper, we will not be overly concerned with the distinctions between those classes of spaces. As we will see in
Theorem

2.59
, pseudo-manifolds, conically stratified spaces, and homotopically stratified sets share a common homotopical property. It is this property - and not their geometrical definitions directly - that is leveraged in the proof of
Theorem

1.2
. We only state results about conically stratified spaces - since it is both a large class, and reasonably easy to define - but
Theorem

1.2
also holds for pseudo-manifolds or homotopically stratified sets.

Homotopy links are known to characterize the stratified homotopy equivalences between homotopically stratified sets, as was shown in
[
Mil13
]
.

Theorem 2.29

(
[
Mil13
, Theorem 6.3]
)
.

A stratified map
f\colon X\to Y\in\mathrm{Top}_{P}
, between two homotopically stratified sets is a stratified homotopy equivalence if and only if, the induced maps of spaces

•

X^{p}\to Y^{p}
,

•

\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{p<q}(X)\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{p<q}(Y)
,

are homotopy equivalences, for all
p\in P
and
q\in P
with
p<q
.

It is also possible to get a similar statement, while only asking the maps between strata and homotopy links to be
weak
-equivalences. In this case, one has to ask that
X
and
Y
are realizations of stratified simplicial sets, (see
[
Dou21c
, Theorem 5,4]
). In any case, these results suggest that homotopy links are a good starting point to build a general homotopy theory of stratified spaces. On the other hand, consider the following example.

Example 2.30
.

Consider the stratified simplex
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
for the (regular) flag
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}<p_{1}<p_{2}]
in
P
as well as its boundary
\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\subset\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
with the induced stratification (see
Fig.

3
). The inclusion

i\colon{}{}|\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}\hookrightarrow{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}

induces homotopy equivalences on all strata and pairwise homotopy links, since for both spaces all of these are contractible. However,
i
is clearly not a stratified homotopy equivalence since if we forget the stratification the underlying map of topological spaces is not even a weak equivalence. Note that this is not in contradiction to
Theorem

2.29
as
{}{}|\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}
is not a homopically stratified set. Indeed, the natural map
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P})\to({}{}|\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P})_{p_{1}}
, for
\mathcal{I}=\{p_{1}<p_{2}\}
, is not a fibration as it has nonempty source but is not surjective.
If we want the stratified homotopy type to be finer than the homotopy type of the underlying space,
\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
and
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
need to have a distinct stratified homotopy type, which can not be defined through strata and homotopy links alone.

On the other hand, note that the homotopy links of
Definition

2.27
can be equivalently defined as
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X)=\mathcal{C}^{0}_{P}({}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P},X)
, for
\mathcal{I}=\{p<q\}
a regular flag of
P
. Note that this definition readily extends to
arbitrary
regular flags
\mathcal{I}=\{p_{0}<\dots<p_{n}\}
. In particular, for the example at hand, one checks that the inclusion
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P})\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P})
is not a weak equivalence for
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{J}
. Indeed, the domain is empty, as there exists no stratified maps
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}\to{}{}|\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}
, while the codomain contains the identity map. This illustrates the necessity to consider
generalized homotopy links
. Indeed, at least when restricting to stratified spaces that admit (stratified) triangulations, stratified maps inducing weak equivalences between all strata and generalized homotopy links are weak equivalence of the underlying spaces (see
Examples

2.45
and
2.46
).

Figure 3.
The simplex
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
and its boundary
\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
, with
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}<p_{1}<p_{2}]
. The first has a non-empty
\mathcal{J}
-holink, while
\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{J}}(\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})=\emptyset
. For all other flags
\mathcal{I}\not=\mathcal{J}
,
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
and
\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
have equivalent
\mathcal{I}
-holinks.

2.5.
Generalized links and homotopy links

We now turn to the more general notion of homotopy links, that was suggested in
Example

2.30
. Note that we will simply call those
homotopy links
, instead of generalized homotopy links, and refer to the homotopy links of Quinn as
classical homotopy links
. Since the latter is just a particular case of the former, as pointed out in
Example

2.30
, this should not cause too much confusion.

Definition 2.31
.

Let
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
,
X\in\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
,
Y\in\mathrm{Top}_{P}
and
\mathcal{I}=[p_{0}<\dots<p_{n}]
a regular flag. We define
\mathcal{I}
-th
homotopy links
, in each of the three categories, as follows:

•

\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)=\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},K)\in\mathrm{sSet}
,

•

\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X)=\textbf{C}^{0}_{N(P)}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)},X)\in\mathrm{Top}
,

•

\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(Y)=\textbf{C}^{0}_{P}({}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P},Y)\in\mathrm{Top}
.

When there is a need to distinguish between them, the first two will be respectively called the
simplicial homotopy link
and the
strong homotopy link
, while the last one will always just be called the homotopy link.

Remark 2.32
.

There is a simple mnemonic underlying the choice of font for
\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}
,
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}
. Vertices of
\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)
are given by simplicial maps from a stratified simplex into
K
. Meanwhile, elements of
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X)
are given by strongly stratum preserving maps from (the realization) of a stratified simplex into a space
X
and elements of
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(Y)
by stratum preserving ones. Hence, the degree of rigidity required from these maps decreases in the order of listing, see
Fig.

2
. This is reflected by the font used for the "Ho" part of the respective notation.

Remark 2.33
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Top}_{P}
be a
P
-stratified space and
\mathcal{I}
a regular flag. Then, by
2.13
and
2.18
we have natural isomorphisms:

\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}\big(\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)\big)\cong\mathrm{Sing}\big(\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X)\big)\cong\mathrm{Sing}\big(\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X\times_{P}{}|N(P)|)\big).

Before considering a converse statement with
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, we first introduce a generalization of the classical notion of links of sub-complex of a simplicial complex.

Definition 2.34
.

Let
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
be a stratified simplicial set, and
\mathcal{I}
a regular flag.
The
\mathcal{I}
-th simplicial link of
K
,
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(K)}
, is defined via the following pullback diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(K)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}{(K)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}(N(P))\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}(\varphi_{K})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathcal{I}}}

where
\mathrm{sd}
is the usual barycentric subdivision, and
i_{\mathcal{I}}
is the map sending the point to the unique vertex in
\mathrm{sd}(N(P))
corresponding to the regular flag
\mathcal{I}
. This construction induces a functor

\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{}:\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\to\mathrm{sSet}.

Remark 2.35
.

In the case when
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
comes from a simplicial complex and
P=\mathcal{I}=\{p_{0}<p_{1}\}
, the simplicial set
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(K)}
is the simplicial link of the
p_{0}
stratum in
K
. In other words, it is the complex obtained by taking the boundary of the simplicial complex given by simplices which contain a vertex in the
p_{0}
stratum. As already noted in
[
Qui88
]
, from this it follows that
{}|\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(K)}|
and
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})
are weakly equivalent. One of the key result in this paper (
Theorem

1.4
), is that this generalizes to arbitrary regular flags and stratified simplicial sets, and to all the notions of homotopy links considered here.

Definition 2.36
.

Let
\varphi_{K}\colon K\to N(P)
be a stratified simplicial set,
\mathcal{I}
a regular flag and
b\in{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
be the barycenter. Let
{}|K|_{b}\subset{}|K|
be the subspace given by
{}|\varphi_{K}|^{-1}(b)
. In other words,
{}|K|_{b}
is the space defined via the following pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|K|_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|K|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{b\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|N(P)|\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\varphi_{K}|}

Remark 2.37
.

One can also think of
{}|K|_{b}
as a
geometric link
(see,
[
Mil68
, Theorem 2.10]
). Consider the case where
P=\{0<1\}=\mathcal{I}
, with the identification
{}|N(P)|\simeq[0,1]
, and
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
is a triangulation of some pseudo-manifold with an isolated singularity. Then,
{}|\varphi_{X}|_{N(P)}^{-1}([0,1[)
is a neighborhood of the singular point, and must be isomorphic to some cone,
c(L)
, for some compact manifold
L
. The subspace
{}|K|_{b}={}|\varphi_{X}|_{N(P)}^{-1}(\frac{1}{2})
is then homeomorphic to the subspace
L\times\{\frac{1}{2}\}
, or in other words, to the link. See
Fig.

1
for a side by side comparison between
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)
and
{}|K|_{b}
.

Let
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
be a stratified simplicial set,
\mathcal{I}
a regular flag and
b\in{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
the barycenter. We will be interested in the following maps:

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|K|_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}

The map labeled
*
is a non-natural homeomorphism and will be defined at the begining of
Section

4
,
Proposition

4.1
. On the other hand the other maps are both easily defined, and seen to be natural. Using the structure described so far, the first map is equivalently given as a map
\mathrm{Map}(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},X)=\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(X)\to\mathrm{Sing}(\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|X|_{N(P)}))=\mathrm{Map}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)},{}|X|_{N(P)})
. It is the map sending a map
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\Delta^{n}\to X
to its realization. The second map just corresponds to the inclusion
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{N(P)}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)},{}|X|_{N(P)})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}_{N(P)}({}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P},{}{}|X|_{P})
. Finally, the third map is given by evaluating maps of the form
{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}\to{}|X|_{N(P)}
at
b\in{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
.
Section

4
is devoted to proving that the second and the third map in Diagram (
3
) are weak equivalences, while
Section

6
handles the first map (or rather, equivalently, it deals with the composition of the first and the second).

2.6.
Homotopy links and diagrams

Recollection 2.38
.

Let
K
be a stratified simplicial set, and
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
two regular flags. The inclusion
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\to\Delta^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
induces a map
\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}(K)\to\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)
. In fact, the data of the holinks can be synthesized as the following diagram

\displaystyle R(P)^{op}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{sSet}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(K),

where
R(P)
is the category of regular
P
-flags (see
Definition

2.5
). We write
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}=\mathrm{Fun}(R(P)^{op},\mathrm{sSet})
for the category of such diagrams. Note that
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
can equivalently be described as the category of presheaves over
R(P)\times\Delta
, i.e.
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}\cong\mathrm{Fun}((R(P)\times\Delta)^{op},\text{Set})
, though we will mostly consider objects of
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
as diagrams of simplicial sets.

We have a functor

\displaystyle D_{P}\colon\mathrm{sSet}_{P}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Diag}_{P}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\mathcal{I}\mapsto\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)\big)

The functor admits a left adjoint,
C_{P}\colon\mathrm{Diag}_{P}\to\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
. Since
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
is a presheaf category over
R(P)\times\Delta
, it is enough to define
C_{P}
on pairs
(\mathcal{I},n)\in R(P)\times\Delta
and then take the left Kan extension along the Yoneda embedding
R(P)\times\Delta\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
. On
R(P)\times\Delta
, one defines
C_{P}(\mathcal{I},n)=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\Delta^{n}
. A more explicit definition can be found in
[
Dou21b
, Definition 2.5]
. Similarily, there are functors
D^{\mathrm{Top}}_{P}\colon\mathrm{Top}_{P}\to\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
, as well as
D^{\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}}_{P}\colon\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}\to\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
defined as follows

\displaystyle D^{\mathrm{Top}}_{P}\colon\mathrm{Top}_{P}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Diag}_{P}

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\mathcal{I}\mapsto\mathrm{Sing}(\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(Y))\big)

\displaystyle D^{\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}}_{P}\colon\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Diag}_{P}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\mathcal{I}\mapsto\mathrm{Sing}(\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X))\big)

D_{P}^{\mathrm{Top}}
and
D^{\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}}_{P}
both admit left adjoints,
C_{P}^{\mathrm{Top}}\colon\mathrm{Diag}_{P}\to\mathrm{Top}_{P}
, and
C_{P}^{\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}}\colon\mathrm{Diag}_{P}\to\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
. Note that the six functors defined above satisfy several relations, which can be summed up by the following diagram of adjunctions:

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sSet}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Diag}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Top}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|-|_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|-|_{N(P)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{P}\circ-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sing}_{N(P)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{P}^{\mathrm{Top}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sing}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{P}^{\mathrm{Top}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\times_{P}{}|N(P)|}

In the above diagram, all pairs of parallel maps are adjoint pairs. Furthermore if one restrict to left adjoints, then one gets a commutative diagram (up to natural isomorphisms), and similarily for the right adjoints. Given the compatibility between all those functors, we will mostly omit the superscript
\mathrm{Top}
and
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
.

The values of the functors
D_{P}
on stratified objects compile all the information about their (generalized) homotopy links. This suggests that diagrams form a reasonable basis to build stratified homotopy types from.

2.7.
The model category of diagrams

Recall that any category of functors into a model category admits a projective model structure. Note that throughout the paper, we consider the category of simplicial sets with the Kan-Quillen model structure.

Theorem 2.39

(
[
Hir09
, Theorem 11.6.1]
)
.

There exists a projective model structure on
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}=\mathrm{Fun}(R(P)^{op},\mathrm{sSet})
, such that:

•

fibrations are maps
f\colon F\to G
, such that
f(\mathcal{I})\colon F(\mathcal{I})\to G(\mathcal{I})
is a Kan fibration for all
\mathcal{I}\in R(P)
,

•

weak equivalences are maps
f\colon F\to G
, such that
f(\mathcal{I})\colon F(\mathcal{I})\to G(\mathcal{I})
is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets, for all
\mathcal{I}\in R(P)
.

This model structure is cofibrantly generated, and we need the following definition to make explicit the sets of generating (trivial) cofibrations.

Definition 2.40
.

Let
S
be a simplicial set and
\mathcal{I}
a regular flag. Let
S^{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
be the diagram defined as follows:

S^{\mathcal{I}}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}S&\text{ if $\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{I}$,}\\
\emptyset&\text{ else.}\end{array}\right.

Proposition 2.41
.

The set of generating cofibrations for the model structure of
Theorem

2.39
is given by:

\{(\partial\Delta^{n})^{\mathcal{I}}\to(\Delta^{n})^{\mathcal{I}}\mid n\geq 0,\ \mathcal{I}\in R(P)\}.

The set of generating trivial cofibrations is given by:

\{(\Lambda^{n}_{k})^{\mathcal{I}}\to(\Delta^{n})^{\mathcal{I}}\mid n\geq 1,\ 0\leq k\leq n,\ \mathcal{I}\in R(P)\}.

Where
\Lambda^{n}_{k}
is the
k
-th horn on
\Delta^{n}
, i.e.
\Lambda^{n}_{k}=\cup_{i\not=k}d_{i}(\Delta^{n})\subset\Delta^{n}
.

It is difficult to give an explicit description of the cofibrant objects in a projective model structure in general, but in the case of
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
there exists a simple characterization. Since cofibrant diagrams are related to the vertical objects we will use in
Section

6.2
(see
Remark

6.16
), we give such a description here.

Proposition 2.42
.

Assume that
P
is of finite length, and let
F\in\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
be a diagram. Then
F
is cofibrant if and only if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(1)

For all regular flags
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, the map
F(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})\to F(\mathcal{I})
is a monomorphism,

(2)

If
\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{2}
are two regular flags such that
\mathcal{I}_{0}=\mathcal{I}_{1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{2}\not=\emptyset
, then either

•

\mathcal{I}_{3}=\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}
is a flag, and
F(\mathcal{I}_{1})\cap F(\mathcal{I}_{2})=F(\mathcal{I}_{3})\subset F(\mathcal{I}_{0})
,

•

or
\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}
is not a flag, and
F(\mathcal{I}_{1})\cap F(\mathcal{I}_{2})=\emptyset
.

Proof.

We give a brief sketch of the proof. For the direct implication, note that a projectively cofibrant diagram must satisfy Condition (
1
) whenever the indexing category is a poset. For Condition (
2
), one easily checks that it is satisfied by the domains and codomains of generating cofibrations. Then, it is a matter of checking that this property is preserved by taking disjoint unions, pushouts and retracts.

For the reverse implication, consider the following characterization of projectively cofibrant diagrams of simplicial sets
[
Dug01
, Corollary 9.4]
. A diagram
F\colon R(P)^{op}\to\mathrm{sSet}
is projectively cofibrant if and only if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(a)

For all
n\geq 0
, the functor of
n
-simplicies
F_{n}\colon R(P)^{op}\to\text{Set}
splits into two sub-functors
F_{n}=F_{n}^{\text{degen}}\coprod F_{n}^{\text{non-degen}}
where the former reaches exactly the degenerate
n
-simplices of
F(\mathcal{I})
, for all
\mathcal{I}
.

(b)

For all
n\geq 0
,
F_{n}
can be further decomposed into a coproduct of representables.

We will first prove that
\eqref{item:Cofibrant_Diagrams_Mono}\Rightarrow\ref{item:Dugger_b}
. Let
\sigma\in F(\mathcal{I})_{n}
for some
\mathcal{I}\in R(P)
. Define the set
F_{\sigma}\subset\coprod_{\mathcal{I}}F(\mathcal{I})_{n}
as the smallest subset such that

•

\sigma\in F_{\sigma}
,

•

\forall\ \mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\in R(P)
, if
\tau\in F(\mathcal{I})\cap F_{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{I}
, then the image of
\tau
in
F(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
is in
F_{\sigma}

•

\forall\ \mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\in R(P)
, if
\tau\in F(\mathcal{I})
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{I}
, and the image of
\tau
in
F(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
is in
F_{\sigma}
, then
\tau
is in
F_{\sigma}
.

First note that by construction, if
\sigma\in F(\mathcal{I})
and
\tau\in F(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
, then either
F_{\sigma}\cap F_{\tau}=\emptyset
or
F_{\sigma}=F_{\tau}
. Next, by (
1
), if
\sigma
is non-degenerate, then so are all the simplices in
F_{\sigma}
, since cofibrations send non-degenerate simplices to non-degenerate simplices, and similarly, if
\sigma
is degenerate all simplices in
F_{\sigma}
are degenerate. Now, note that
F_{\sigma}
defines a subfunctor of
F_{n}
by setting
F_{\sigma}(\mathcal{I})=F_{\sigma}\cap F(\mathcal{I})
.
To prove that
\eqref{item:Cofibrant_Diagrams_Mono}+\eqref{item:Cofibrant_Diagrams_square}\Rightarrow\ref{item:Dugger_a}
, it is enough to show that the
F_{\sigma}
defined above are representable. Let
Q_{\sigma}=\{(\tau,\mathcal{I})\mid\tau\in F_{\sigma}(\mathcal{I})\}
, and equip it with the order relation
(\tau_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1})<(\tau_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2})
if
\mathcal{I}_{1}\subset\mathcal{I}_{2}
and the image of
\tau_{2}
in
F(\mathcal{I}_{1})
is
\tau_{1}
. Then, by construction of
F_{\sigma}
, the poset
Q_{\sigma}
is connected.
In particular, if
(\tau,\mathcal{I})
and
(\tau^{\prime},\mathcal{I})
are two elements of
Q_{\sigma}
, then they must be related by a zigzag. Note that by (
2
), any zigzag of the form
(\tau_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1})>(\tau_{0},\mathcal{I}_{0})<(\tau_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2})
can be replaced by a zigzag
(\tau_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1})<(\tau_{3},\mathcal{I}_{3})>(\tau_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2})
. Then, by repeated application of (
2
), one can assume that there exists
(\tau^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
, such that,
(\tau,\mathcal{I})<(\tau^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})>(\tau^{\prime},\mathcal{I})
, which implies that
\tau=\tau^{\prime}
. This has two consequences. First,
Q_{\sigma}
is a subposet of
R(P)^{op}
, and second,
F_{\sigma}(\mathcal{I})
is either empty or is a point. To show that
F_{\sigma}
is reprensentable, it remains to be showed that
Q_{\sigma}
admits a maximal element. Once again, by repeated application of (
2
), any two elements
(\tau_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1}),(\tau_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2})
must admit an upper bound
(\tau_{3},\mathcal{I}_{3})\geq(\tau_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1}),(\tau_{2},\mathcal{I}_{2})
, but now,
\mathcal{I}_{3}
is a regular flag of length greater or equal than the length of
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
, with possible equality only if
\mathcal{I}_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{2}
. Since
P
is assumed to be of finite length, the length of regular flags is bounded, and thus
Q_{\sigma}
admits a maximal element,
\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}
. The functor
F_{\sigma}\colon R(P)^{op}\to\text{Set}
is then represented by
\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}
.
∎

2.8.
Model categories for stratified spaces

We can now describe the model category of stratified spaces as introduced in
[
Dou21b
]
.

Theorem 2.43

(
[
Dou21b
, Theorem 2.15]
)
.

There exists a model structure on
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
, where a map
f\colon X\to Y
is

•

a fibration if the induced maps
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X)\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(Y)
are fibrations for all
\mathcal{I}\in R(P)
,

•

a weak equivalence if the induced maps
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X)\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(Y)
are weak equivalences for all
\mathcal{I}\in R(P)
.

This model structure is cofibrantly generated and the set of generating cofibrations is given by

\{{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}\times S^{n-1}\hookrightarrow{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}\times B^{n}\mid n\geq 0,\ \mathcal{I}\in R(P)\},

while the set of generating trivial cofibrations is given by

\{{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}\times B^{n}\times\{0\}\hookrightarrow{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}\times B^{n}\times[0,1]\mid n\geq 0,\ \mathcal{I}\in R(P)\},

where
B^{n}
and
S^{n-1}
are respectively the
n
-dimensional ball and the
(n-1)
-dimensional sphere bounding it.

Remark 2.44
.

The model category
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
has the notable property that all of its objects are fibrant, which is a property also enjoyed by the classical model structure on
\mathrm{Top}
. To see this, note that for all
\mathcal{I}\in R(P)
,
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(P)=*
, in particular, for all
X\in\mathrm{Top}_{P}
, the map
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X)\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(P)=*
is a fibration in
\mathrm{Top}
, which implies that the map
X\to P
is a fibration in
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
.

Example 2.45
.

For arbitrary stratified spaces in
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
it is not the case that the forgetful functor
\mathrm{Top}_{P}\to\mathrm{Top}
preserves weak equivalences. Indeed, let
E
be the Hawaiian Earring (see for ex.
[
EK00
]
) and
f:E^{\prime}=\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}S^{1}\to E
the bijection obtained by decomposing
H
into its circles, and gluing these along the basepoint.
f
is far from being a weak-equivalence of topological spaces. Indeed,
[
EK00
, Thm. 3.1]
shows that it does not even induce an isomorphism on singular homology in degree
1
. Nevertheless, if we consider
E
and
E^{\prime}
as stratified over
P=\{0<1\}
, by sending the singular point to
0
, then
f
induces a weak equivalence of stratified spaces. Indeed, it even induces a homeomorphism on strata and homotopy links.
This should not be excessively surprising. The forgetful functor
\mathrm{Top}_{P}\to\mathrm{Top}
is left Quillen, which means it is only expected to preserve weak equivalences between
cofibrant
objects. However, not every object in
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
, in particular not the Hawaiian Earring, is cofibrant.

Remark 2.46
.

Despite
Example

2.45
, it holds true that the forgetful functor
\mathrm{Top}_{P}\to\mathrm{Top}
preserves weak equivalences for much larger class than that of cofibrant objects. In fact, it preserves weak equivalences between realizations of stratified simplicial sets. This is a consequence of
Theorem

5.1
, together with the fact that
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\to\mathrm{sSet}
preserves weak equivalences, by a direct application of Ken Browns Lemma (
[
Hir09
, Cor. 7.7.2]
.

Remark 2.47
.

The model structure defined above was originally by transporting the model structure from
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
along the adjunction
C_{P}\colon\mathrm{Diag}_{P}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Top}_{P}\colon D_{P}
(see
2.38
). This uses the transport theorem
[
HKRS17
, Corollary 3.3.4]
, and this is were the necessity to restrict to
\Delta
-generated spaces arose, since the theorem requires
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
to be locally presentable (see
Remark

2.10
). Using the alternative transfer theorem
[
Ste13
, A.1]
instead, one can prove the existence of an identically defined model structure on the category of
P
-stratified spaces defined using
all
topological spaces.
Denote the larger model category constructed in this fashion by
\mathcal{T}_{P}
. Then
\mathcal{T}_{P}
has identically defined acyclic cofibrations and fibrations as
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
. In particular, the adjunction

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{P}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Top}_{P}

given by inclusion and
\Delta
-generation is a Quillen-adjunction. It follows immediately from the universal property of
\Delta
-generation and the definition of homotopy links that both unit and counit of this adjunction are weak equivalences. In particular, the two model categories are Quillen-equivalent. Hence, from the perspective of homotopy theory, the restriction to
\Delta
-generated spaces can be neglected. At the same time,
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
enjoys the additional benefit of being a combinatorial model category enriched over
\mathrm{Top}
, (and over
\mathrm{sSet}
, through the functor
\mathrm{Sing}
) making it generally preferable to work with. Even more, one easily checks that with its simplicial structure described in
2.13
,
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
is a simplicial model category.

There is a similar model structure for
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
, also obtained by transporting along the adjunction with
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
.

Theorem 2.48

(
[
Dou21b
, Theorem 2.8]
)
.

There exists a model structure on
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
, where a map
f\colon X\to Y
is

•

a fibration if the induced maps
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X)\to\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(Y)
are fibrations for all
\mathcal{I}\in R(P)
,

•

a weak equivalence if the induced maps
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X)\to\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(Y)
are weak equivalences for all
\mathcal{I}\in R(P)
.

This model structure is cofibrantly generated and the set of generating cofibrations is given by

\{{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}\times S^{n-1}\hookrightarrow{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}\times B^{n}\mid n\geq 0,\ \mathcal{I}\in R(P)\},

while the set of generating trivial cofibrations is given by

\{{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}\times B^{n}\times\{0\}\hookrightarrow{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}\times B^{n}\times[0,1]\mid n\geq 0,\ \mathcal{I}\in R(P)\},

where
B^{n}
and
S^{n-1}
are respectively the
n
-dimensional ball and the
(n-1)
-dimensional sphere bounding it.

The model categories from
Theorems

2.43
and
2.48
are Quillen-equivalent, through the adjunction
\varphi_{P}\circ-\dashv-\times_{P}{}|N(P)|
described in
2.18
, see
[
Dou21b
, Theorem 2.15]
together with the correction in
[
Dou21a
, Theorem 3.15]
for a proof.

Theorem 2.49
.

The adjunction
\varphi_{P}\circ-\colon\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Top}_{P}\colon-\times_{P}{}|N(P)|

is a Quillen-equivalence.

The model categories
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
and
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
are also Quillen-equivalent to
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
.

Theorem 2.50

(
[
Dou21b
, Theorem 2.12]
)
.

The adjunctions
C_{P}\colon\mathrm{Diag}_{P}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}\colon D_{P}
and
C_{P}\colon\mathrm{Diag}_{P}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Top}_{P}\colon D_{P}
are Quillen-equivalences.

Leveraging the structure described in
Remark

2.12
, one can construct a model structure on
\mathrm{Strat}
through the theory of Quillen-bifibrations, see
[
CM20
]
. Note that any isomorphism of posets
\alpha\colon P\to Q
, induces an equivalence of categories
\mathrm{Diag}_{Q}\to\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
. In particular, given a map in
\mathrm{Strat}

(5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}}

where
\bar{f}
is an isomorphism, one can consider the diagram associated to
Y
as a diagram in
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
, and consider
f
as inducing a map in
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
. From this, it follows that a weak equivalence induces a map of homotopy links.

Theorem 2.51

(
[
Dou21b
, Theorem 3.6]
)
.

There exist a model structure on
\mathrm{Strat}
where a map
f\colon(X\to P)\to(Y\to Q)
is a weak equivalence if and only if
\bar{f}\colon P\to Q
is an isomorphism, and the maps induced by
f

\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X)\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\bar{f}(\mathcal{I})}(Y),

are weak equivalences for all
\mathcal{I}\in R(P)
.

This model category is cofibrantly generated with set of generating cofibrations is given by

\{{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{\mathbb{N}}\times S^{n-1}\hookrightarrow{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{\mathbb{N}}\times B^{n}\mid n\geq 0,\ \mathcal{I}\in R(\mathbb{N})\},

and the set of generating trivial cofibrations is given by

\{{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{\mathbb{N}}\times B^{n}\times\{0\}\hookrightarrow{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{\mathbb{N}}\times B^{n}\times[0,1]\mid n\geq 0,\ \mathcal{I}\in R(\mathbb{N})\}.

2.9.
The model category of stratified simplicial sets

Before defining a model structure for stratified simplicial sets, we need to define a particular set of stratified horn inclusions.

Definition 2.52
.

A
stratified horn inclusion
is an inclusion
\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}\to\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, where
\mathcal{J}
is a flag and
\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}=\cup_{i\not=k}d_{i}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})
, i.e. it is the union of all proper faces of
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
other than its
k
-th face.
An
admissible horn inclusion
is a stratified horn inclusion
\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}\to\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
such that any of the following equivalent condition is satisfied:

•

The map
\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}\to\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
is a stratified homotopy equivalence (see
Definition

2.22
).

•

There exists a flag
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
such that
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
is either the
k
-th or
(k+1)
-th degeneracy of
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
.

•

\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
and
p_{k}=p_{k+1}
or
p_{k}=p_{k-1}
.

For a proof that the above conditions are equivalent, see
[
Dou21c
, Proposition 1.13]
.

Theorem 2.53

(
[
Dou21c
, Theorem 2.14]
)
.

There exists a cofibrantly generated model structure on
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
where the generating set of cofibrations is given by

\{\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\to\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\mid\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\in\Delta(P)\},

and the generating set of trivial cofibrations is given by

\{\iota^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}\colon\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}\to\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\mid\text{$\iota^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}$ is an admissible horn inclusion}\}.

weak equivalences in this category are the maps
f\colon K\to L
such that for all
\mathcal{I}\in R(P)
, the induced maps

(6)

\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(K^{\mathrm{fib}})\to\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(L^{\mathrm{fib}})

are weak equivalences, where
(-)^{\mathrm{fib}}
means passing to a fibrant replacement.

Remark 2.54
.

As is usual for model category of presheaves, all objects of
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
are cofibrant. In fact, the class of cofibrations is exactly the class of monomorphisms in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
. Indeed, the set of generating cofibrations is the set of boundary inclusions of (stratified) simplices, which can easily be seen to generate all monomorphisms.

Remark 2.55
.

Note that stratified homotopy equivalences in the sense of
Definition

2.22
are weak equivalences in the model category described above. Furthermore, this model structure is minimal among model structures satisfying this property and having monomorphisms as cofibrations, i.e. it has the smallest possible class of weak equivalences. This comes from the fact that the model structure of
Theorem

2.53
is built using Cisinski’s theory
[
Cis06
]
. By
[
Cis06
, Théorème 1.3.22]
, such a model category is specified by the data of a cylinder (
Definition

2.22
) and a class of anodyne extension. In the case of
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, since admissible horn inclusions are stratified homotopy equivalences, the class of anodyne extensions is the one generated by the empty set, see
[
Cis06
, 1.3.12, Proposition 1.3.13]
. This implies in particular that if a model structure on
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
is such that

•

the cofibrations are monomorphisms,

•

stratified homotopy equivalences are weak equivalences,

then it is a right Bousfield localization of the structure of
Theorem

2.53
. One particular example of interest is the model structure
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}^{\text{Joyal-Kan}}
defined by Haine in
[
Hai18
]
