. Haine starts from the Joyal model structure, and then localizes the structure along the inclusions of stratified simplicial sets into their cylinders. In particular,
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}^{\text{Joyal-Kan}}
is a right Bousfield localization of
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
.

Remark 2.56
.

Note that, contrary to the case of stratified spaces (
Theorem

2.43
), the weak equivalences for stratified simplicial sets are not
defined
as the maps inducing weak equivalences between homotopy links, but only as those maps satisfying this property
after a suitable fibrant replacement
. In particular, the model structure on
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
is not transported from the category of diagrams
\mathrm{Diag}_{P}
. On the other hand, consider the model structure described by Henriques in
[
Hen
]
, in which weak equivalences are defined as those maps inducing weak equivalences between all simplicial homotopy links. The two model structures are known to be the same for very general reasons (for example, consider the previous remark), but it is not immediately clear why the classes of weak equivalences coincide. We investigate this in
Section

6
, see also
Remark

6.3
.

Similarly to the case of stratified spaces, there is a model structure on all stratified simplicial sets.

Theorem 2.57
.

There exists a model structure on
s\mathrm{Strat}
where a map
f\colon(K\to N(P))\to(L\to N(Q))
is a weak equivalence if and only if
\bar{f}\colon P\to Q
is an isomrophism and the maps induced by
f

\mathrm{HoLink}_{\mathcal{I}}(K^{\mathrm{fib}})\to\mathrm{HoLink}_{\bar{f}(\mathcal{I})}(L^{\mathrm{fib}})

are weak equivalences for all
\mathcal{I}\in R(P)
, where
(-)^{\mathrm{fib}}
is a fibrant replacement in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
.

The model category is cofibrantly generated, and the set of generating cofibrations is given by

\{\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\mid\mathcal{I}\in R(\mathbb{N})\},

and the set of generating trivial cofibrations is given by

\{\iota_{k}^{\mathcal{I}}\colon\Lambda_{k}^{\mathcal{I}}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\mid\text{$\iota^{\mathcal{I}}_{k}$ is admissible in $\mathrm{sSet}_{\mathbb{N}}$}\}.

2.10.
Failure to be a Quillen-equivalence

Now that we have two homotopy theories, one for stratified simplicial sets, and one for stratified spaces, we want to show that they coincide through the adjunction
{}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}}\colon s\mathrm{Strat}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Strat}\colon\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}
. In the language of model categories, this would mean showing that the adjunction is a Quillen-equivalence. However, this adjunction already fails to be a Quillen-adjunction, since, the right adjoint
\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}
does not preserve fibrancy, as we show in the following recollection.

Recollection 2.58
.

Let
X\to P
be a stratified space. For
X
to be fibrant, one has to check that for all regular flags
\mathcal{I}\in R(P)
,
D_{P}(X)(\mathcal{I})
is a Kan-complex. But note that by definition, those are equal to
\mathrm{Sing}(\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X))
, and so, are Kan-complexes, which means that any stratified space is fibrant as already observed in
Remark

2.44
. On the other hand, for
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)
to be fibrant means that in any diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(P)\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,,}}

where
\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}
is admissible, there exists a lift. Using the adjunction
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
, and the fact that
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(P)=N(P)
, this is equivalent to asking for a lift in diagrams of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}|_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,.}}

Now consider the poset
P=\{0<1\}
, and the stratified space
X={}{}|\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{1}|_{P}
with
\mathcal{J}=[0\leq 0\leq 1]
. Then,
\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{1}
is admissible, and so for
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)
to be fibrant, there must be a lift in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{1}|_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{1}|_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

but there exist no stratified section
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}\to{}{}|\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{1}|_{P}
, as illustrated in
Fig.

4
, which means that
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}({}{}|\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{1}|_{P})
is not fibrant. In particular, the functor
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
does not preserve fibrations.

Figure 4.
The inclusion
{}{}|\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{1}|_{P}\hookrightarrow{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}
, with
\mathcal{J}=[0\leq 0\leq 1]
, admits no stratified section, since any section would send some part ot the interior of
{}|\Delta^{2}|
to the singular stratum.

On the other hand, stratified spaces that are in one of the three classes mentioned in
Section

2.4
, pseudo-manifolds, conically stratified spaces and homotopically stratified sets, have better behaved
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
.

Theorem 2.59

(
[
Lur17
, Theorem A.6.4]
[
NL19
, Proposition 8.1.2.6]
)
.

Let
X\to P
be a stratified space. If it is either a conically stratified space or if
P
is finite and
X
is a homotopically stratified set, then the simplicial set underlying
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)
is a quasi-category.

In those cases, this implies that
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)
is a fibrant object in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, see
[
Dou21c
, Proposition 4.12]
. Note that while
2.58
implies that the adjunction
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
is not a Quillen-adjunction, we can still use it to deduce results about objects for which
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}

is
fibrant, such as those mentionned above. In fact it is possible to recover an independent proof of Miller’s Theorem (
Theorem

2.29
), this way (see
[
Dou21c
, Section 5]
).
Relatedly,
Theorem

1.2
(
Corollary

5.8
) states that those objects do behave like fibrant objects in the homotopy category
\mathrm{Ho}\mathrm{Strat}
.

2.11.
The stratified subdivision and its adjoint

As we have seen in the previous section, the model structure on
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
and
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
cannot be compared directly through the adjunction
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
. To achieve a comparison, one needs to work with a suitably defined stratified subdivision. Note that this stratified subdivision was already used to characterize the model structure of
Theorem

2.53
in
[
Dou21c
]
, and that, through its adjoint, it allows for the definition of a fibrant replacement functor in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, see
Sections

3.1
and
3.2
.

Remark 2.60
.

In this subsection and through the remainder of this paper, we will often abuse notation by identifying an
n
-dimensional stratified simplex
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
with its underlying simplex
\Delta^{n}
. This allows for notation such as
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}_{0}
to refer to the set of vertices of
\Delta^{n}
.

Recollection 2.61
.

Denote by
\mathrm{sd}\colon\mathrm{sSet}\to\mathrm{sSet}
the classical barycentric subdivision
[
Kan57
, Section 2]
.
Given a stratified simplex
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
,
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
its stratified subdivision is the subcomplex
\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})\subset\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n})\times\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
, given by such simplices
[(\sigma_{0},q_{0}),\dots,(\sigma_{k},q_{k})]

fulfilling
\{q_{0},\dots,q_{k}\}\subset\{p_{i}\mid i\in\sigma_{0}\}
. It is stratified via projection to the second component. This construction left Kan extends to a functor

\mathrm{sd}_{P}\colon\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\to\mathrm{sSet}_{P}.

For a stratified simplicial set
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, we can also describe the stratified simplicial set
\mathrm{sd}_{P}(K)
more explicitely. Note that we have an inclusion
\mathrm{sd}_{P}(N(P))\subset\mathrm{sd}(N(P))\times N(P)
, composing it with the projection on the first factor gives a map
\mathrm{sd}_{P}(N(P))\to\mathrm{sd}(N(P))
. The stratified simplicial set
\mathrm{sd}_{P}(K)
can then be seen as the following pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}_{P}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}_{P}(N(P))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}(N(P))\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}(\varphi_{K})}

and the stratification is given by the composition
\mathrm{sd}_{P}(K)\to\mathrm{sd}_{P}(N(P))\to N(P)
.

The functor
\mathrm{sd}_{P}
naturally comes with a natural transformation,

\mathrm{l.v}_{P}\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}\to 1_{\mathrm{sSet}_{P}}
, the
stratified last vertex map
.
We define it on stratified simplices by giving its value on vertices:

\displaystyle\mathrm{l.v}_{P}\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})_{0}

\displaystyle\to\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}_{0}

\displaystyle([q_{0}\leq\dots\leq q_{k}],q)

\displaystyle\mapsto\max\{i\mid q_{i}=q\}.

Then, since the above is a map between simplicial complexes, its value on vertices uniquely defines a simplicial map
\mathrm{l.v}_{P}\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})\to\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
.

The functor
\mathrm{sd}_{P}
admits a right adjoint, defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ex}_{P}:\mathrm{sSet}_{P}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{sSet}_{P}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{sSet}_{P}(\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{-}),K)

where again, by
Remark

2.20
, we consider
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
as a category of presheaves over
\Delta(P)
. The adjoints of the maps
\mathrm{l.v}_{P}\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}(K)\to K
give natural maps
\iota_{K}\colon K\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(K)
, which assemble into a natural transformation
\iota\colon 1_{\mathrm{sSet}_{P}}\to\mathrm{Ex}_{P}
.
Finally, we denote by
\mathrm{sd}^{n}_{P}
,
\mathrm{Ex}^{n}_{P}
,
\mathrm{l.v}^{n}_{P},\iota^{n}
the obvious functors obtained by iteration and by
\mathrm{Ex}^{\infty}_{P}
the colimit of the diagram

1_{\mathrm{sSet}_{P}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ex}_{P}\hookrightarrow Ex^{2}_{P}\hookrightarrow\dots.

Given
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
the stratified simplicial set
\mathrm{Ex}^{\infty}_{P}(K)
is fibrant
[
Dou21c
, Lemma 2.10]
. It is the content of
Corollary

3.2
that
X\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ex}^{\infty}_{P}(X)
in fact defines a fibrant replacement of
K
.

Figure 5.
From left to right, the simplex
\Delta^{[0\leq 0\leq 1]}
, its "naive" subdivision, its stratified subdivision
\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{[0\leq 0\leq 1]})
, and the stratified subdivision of
\Delta^{[0\leq 1\leq 2]}

We will also use some properties of the subdivision functor.

Proposition 2.62
.

For all stratified simplicial sets
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, the last vertex map
\mathrm{l.v}_{P}\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}(K)\to K
is a weak equivalence. In particular,
if
f\colon K\to L
is a map in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, then
f
is a weak equivalence if and only if
\mathrm{sd}_{P}(f)
is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

Consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}_{P}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}_{P}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}_{P}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{l.v}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{l.v}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The first part of the proposition asserts that the vertical morphisms are weak equivalences. Thus, by the two out of three law, the first assertion of the proposition implies the second assertion. For the first assertion, consider
[
Dou21c
, Lemma A.3]
, which applies to
\mathrm{sd}_{P}
and
\mathrm{l.v}_{P}
as shown in the proof of
[
Dou21c
, Theorem 2.14]
. One can also prove this directly by checking that it holds on representables, then using the cube lemma in an inductive argument.
∎

2.12.
Recovering a Quillen-equivalence

Composing the adjoint pairs
\mathrm{sd}_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Ex}_{P}
and
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
, one gets a Quillen-equivalence.

Theorem 2.63

(
[
Dou21a
, Theorem 1.15]
)
.

The adjoint pair

(7)

{}{}|\mathrm{sd}_{P}(-)|_{P}\colon\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Top}_{P}\colon\mathrm{Ex}_{P}\mathrm{Sing}_{P}

is a Quillen-equivalence.

In particular, this means that the homotopy theories of spaces and simplicial sets stratified over
P
coincide.

Remark 2.64
.

The Quillen-equivalence obtained above is only partially satisfactory for several reasons.

•

First, the adjunction (
7
) is not very well suited for the study of conically stratified objects, since even reasonable PL-pseudomanifolds are not cofibrant objects of
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
. In particular, they are not in the image of
{}{}|\mathrm{sd}_{P}(-)|_{P}
. Thus, it makes it difficult to relate the homotopy theory of those classical objects with that of stratified spaces. On the other hand, by working with the
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
adjunction, we are able to show that the classical homotopy theory of conically stratified spaces embeds fully faithfully in that of stratified spaces (
Corollary

5.8
). The proof relies on the fact that triangulated conically stratified spaces are in the image of
{}{}|-|_{P}
and have fibrant
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
. This illustrates the usefulness of working with the more natural adjunction
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
.

•

The functors
\mathrm{sd}_{P}
and
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}
are not at all compatible with the Quillen-adjunctions
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{sSet}_{Q}
, induced by maps of posets
\alpha\colon P\to Q
. In particular, one cannot recover a global adjunction
s\mathrm{Strat}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Strat}
by gluing the adjunctions (
7
) for all
P
. This means that in order to compare the homotopy theory of all stratified spaces and stratified simplicial sets, then one needs to work directly with the adjunction
{}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}
.

•

Finally, the stratified setting is very similar to the classical setting. In the latter, the
{}|-|\dashv\mathrm{Sing}
adjunction is already a Quillen-equivalence, with no need for subdivision. Given the fact that the homotopy theory associated to
s\mathrm{Strat}
and
\mathrm{Strat}
can actually be related in a meaningful way (
Theorem

5.1
), through the
{}|-|_{\mathrm{Strat}}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{\mathrm{Strat}}
adjunction, the appearance of
\mathrm{sd}_{P}
and
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}
in (
7
) can appear as artificial.

With that said,
Theorem

2.63
can be seen as the first step to obtain a comparison of the topological and simplicial setting through the
{}{}|-|_{P}\dashv\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
adjunction. Since the latter adjunction is not a Quillen-adjunction, we will need to work with categories with weak equivalences instead of the full model structures, see
Section

5
. In particular, we will need to prove that the functors
{}{}|-|_{P}
and
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
characterize all weak equivalences (
Corollaries

4.22
and
3.16
). By that, we mean that those functor preserve and reflect weak equivalences. By
Theorem

2.63
, this is already known for the functors
{}{}|\mathrm{sd}_{P}(-)|_{P}
and
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
, since they are part of a Quillen-equivalence and all objects of
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
are cofibrant and all objects of
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
are fibrant. Thus, it suffices to show that for any
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
,
{}{}|\mathrm{sd}_{P}(K)|_{P}\to{}{}|K|_{P}
is a weak equivalence, (see
Section

4
, and the proof of
Corollary

4.22
), and that for any
X\in\mathrm{Top}_{P}
, the map
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)\to\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X))
is a weak equivalence (
Proposition

3.1
).

3.
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}
and
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
characterize weak equivalences

In the category of simplicial sets, an explicit and particularly convenient fibrant replacement functor is given by the Kan fibrant replacement
S\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ex}^{\infty}(S)
(see
[
Kan57
,
GJ12
]
). The inclusion map is a trivial cofibration
S\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ex}^{\infty}(S)
which can be obtained (through transfinite composition) by glueing on simplices along horns (see
[
Mos19
]
). In this section, we show that the same can be said for
\mathrm{Ex}^{\infty}_{P}
, in particular that it gives a fibrant replacement functor in the category
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
. From this, we then also obtain that the functor
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}:\mathrm{Top}_{P}\to\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
characterizes weak equivalences.

3.1.
Fibrant replacement a la Kan, in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}

The main content of this subsection is proving the following result.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
{K}\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
be a stratified simplicial set, then the map
{K}\to\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(K)
is a trivial cofibration.

Together with the results from
[
Dou21c
]
, we will then obtain as an immediate consequence:

Corollary 3.2
.

The functor
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\infty}\colon\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\to\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
is a fibrant replacement functor.

We prove
Proposition

3.1
by adapting a proof of the equivalent statement in the non-stratified case from
[
Mos19
]
.
This relies on the notion of a stratified strong anodyne extension, already used in
[
Dou21c
]
.
Some intermediary results of
[
Mos19
]
immediately extend to the stratified case and their proofs will be omitted. We refer the reader to the proof of
[
Mos19
, Theorem 22]
for more details.
Nevertheless, a significant amount of technical preparations is required to replicate the necessary arguments in the stratified framework.

Definition 3.3
.

A
(stratified) strong anodyne extension, (S)SAE
for short, is a morphism
{A}\hookrightarrow{B}
in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, that is given by a transfinite composition of cobase changes of admissible horn inclusions.

For the sake of brevity, we are just going to omit the
stratified
and just refer to SAEs from here on out.

Remark 3.4
.

By definition, all SAEs are trivial cofibrations. In particular, an SAE
{A}\to{B}
is a monomorphism, and can be safely identified with the inclusion of a (stratified) sub-simplicial set.

Strong anodyne extensions enjoy an entirely combinatorial characterization, given as follows. Given a stratified simplicial set
{K}
, let
K_{\mathrm{n.d.}}
be its set of non-degenerate simplices.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
m\colon{A}\hookrightarrow{B}
be a cofibration in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
.

(1)

A
pairing
on
m
is a partition of
B_{n.d.}\setminus A_{n.d.}
into two sets
B_{I}
and
B_{II}
together with a bijection
T:B_{II}\to B_{I}
. The elements of
B_{I}
and
B_{II}
are referred to as
type I
and
type II
simplices respectively.

Now let
T\colon B_{II}\to B_{I}
be a pairing on
m
.

(2)

T
is called
proper
if for each
\sigma\in B_{II}
,
\sigma
is a codimension one face of
T(\sigma)
in a unique way.

(3)

T
is called
admissible
, if in addition, for any type II simplex,
\sigma
, such that
T(\sigma)\colon\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\to B
, and
\sigma=d_{k}(T(\sigma))
,
\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}\to\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
is an admissible horn inclusion.

(4)

Given a pairing
T
on
m
, the
ancestral relation
is the transitive binary relation on
B_{II}
generated by
\sigma\prec_{T}\tau
if
\sigma\not=\tau
and
\sigma
is a face of
T(\tau)

(5)

T
is called
regular
if the ancestral relation
\prec_{T}
is well founded.

Proposition 3.6
.

A cofibration
{A}\hookrightarrow{B}
in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
is a SAE if and only if it admits an admissible proper regular pairing.

Proof.

The proof of
[
Mos19
, Proposition 12]
directly generalizes. The extra admissibility hypothesis guarantees that only admissible horns are being filled.
∎

Now, since SAEs are trivial cofibrations, we can prove
Proposition

3.1
by exhibiting a suitable pairing. We will achieve this by decomposing the map
{K}\to\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(K)
into two maps, and exhibiting a presentation for each of the factors.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
{K}\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
be a stratified simplicial set. Its naive subdivision is
\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(K)=(\mathrm{sd}(K),\varphi_{K}\circ\mathrm{l.v})
. This defines a functor

\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}\colon\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\to\mathrm{sSet}_{P}

which admits a right adjoint,
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}\colon\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\to\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
. The later can also be described as a simplicial subset
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)\subset\mathrm{Ex}(K)

\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)=\{\sigma\colon\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})\to K\ |\ \text{$\sigma$ is stratum preserving}\}

Now, let
\mathcal{J}
be some flag
[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
, define the following map on vertices:

\displaystyle t_{\mathcal{J}}\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})

\displaystyle(q_{0}\leq\dots\leq q_{m},r)

\displaystyle\mapsto(q_{0}\leq\dots\leq q_{l})

where
q_{l}=r
and
q_{l+1}>r
. This extends to a map of stratified simplicial sets,
t_{\mathcal{J}}\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})\to\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})
, and further into a natural transformation
t\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}\to\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}
.

Note that the natural transformation
t\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}\to\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}
fits into a commutative diagram

(8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathrm{sSet}_{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{l.v}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{l.v}}

which, by adjunction, gives the following diagram

(9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathrm{sSet}_{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ex}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}}
,

where the horizontal arrow is the map of interest. We will show that the maps to, and from
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}
are SAEs. In particular, this will imply that their composition is an SAE, which in turn implies
Proposition

3.1
. We start by showing that they are cofibrations.

Lemma 3.8
.

All natural transformations in Diagram (
9
) are cofibrations.

Proof.

It suffices to show, that all of the natural transformations in (
8
) are epimorphisms of simplicial sets when evaluated at
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
. For
\mathrm{l.v}
this is well known. Thus, by closedness of epimorphisms under composition, it remains to show that
t_{\mathcal{J}}
is an epimorphism, for each flag
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
. Label
e_{0},\dots,e_{n}
the vertices of
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
, and let
\sigma=(\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{k})
be a simplex in
\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})
. For
0\leq i\leq k
, let

q_{i}:=\max\{p_{j}\mid e_{j}\in\sigma_{i}\}
.
Note that
q_{i}
specifies the stratum to which the vertex
\sigma_{i}
of
\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})
belongs. For
q\in P
, denote
j_{q}=\min\{i\ |\ q_{i}=q\}
. Next, define
\widetilde{\sigma}\subset\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
as
\widetilde{\sigma}=\{e_{i}\ |\ e_{i}\in\sigma_{j_{p_{i}}}\}
. In other words,
\widetilde{\sigma}
is the simplex given by such vertices
e
, which lie in the smallest
\sigma_{i}
which, considered as a simplex of
\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})
, has the same stratum as
e
. Note, that
\widetilde{\sigma}
is built such that for each
q_{i}
it contain some vertex of stratum
q_{i}
. In particular,

\sigma^{\prime}=((\sigma_{0}\cup\widetilde{\sigma},q_{0}),\dots,(\sigma_{k}\cup\widetilde{\sigma},q_{k}))

defines a simplex of
\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})
. An elementary computation shows that
t(\sigma^{\prime})=\sigma
.
∎

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
{K}\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
be a stratified simplicial set, the map
{K}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)
is an SAE.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.8
we already know that it is a cofibration, and so we need to exhibit a proper regular admissible pairing. Consider the (non-stratified) composition

{K}\to\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)\to\mathrm{Ex}(K).

We know of a proper regular pairing for the composition from
[
Mos19
, Theorem 22]
. By
Lemma

3.10
, it is enough to show that this pairing correctly restricts to
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)
, and that the restricted pairing is admissible. Note that in
[
Mos19
]
, the pairing
T
is defined by pre-composing with certain maps
r^{k}_{n}\colon\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n+1})\to\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n})
, for
0\leq k\leq n
defined on vertices as follows (see
[
Mos19
, Definition 25]
, the pairing is defined immediatly afterwards). First, for vertices of
\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n+1})
of the form
\{i\}
, one has :

r^{k}_{n}(\{i\})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\{i\}&\text{ if $0\leq i\leq k$}\\
\{0,\dots,k\}&\text{ if $i=k+1$}\\
\{i-1\}&\text{ if $i>k+1$}\end{array}\right.

And, then for an arbitrary vertex
\sigma\in\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n+1})
, one has

r^{k}_{n}(\sigma)=\bigcup\limits_{i\in\sigma}r^{k}_{n}(\{i\})

Now let
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
be some flag, and
\mathcal{J}^{k}
the flag obtained by repeating
p_{k}
. Then, note that
r^{k}_{n}
gives a stratum preserving map

r^{k}_{n}\colon\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{k}})\to\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})

Now, if
f\colon\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n})\to K
is some simplex of type II which happens to be in
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)
, then there is some flag
\mathcal{J}
such that
f\colon\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})\to K
is a stratum preserving map. Furthermore, one has
T(f)=f\circ r^{k}_{n}
for some
0\leq k\leq n
, but then,
f\circ r^{k}_{n}\colon\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{k}})\to{K}
is also a stratum preserving map. Which implies that
T
restricts to a proper and regular pairing to the inclusion
{K}\to\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)
. Finally, the pairing is admissible since the horn inclusion
\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}^{k}}_{k}\to\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{k}}
is admissible, by definition of
\mathcal{J}^{k}
.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

Suppose one is given two cofibrations of simplicial sets
m_{1}:B_{0}\hookrightarrow B_{1}
,
m_{2}:B_{1}\hookrightarrow B_{2}
. Then, a proper regular pairing
T
on
m_{2}\circ m_{1}
restricts to proper regular pairings on
m_{1}
and on
m_{2}
if and only if we have
T(B_{II}\cap B_{1,n.d.})\subset B_{1,n.d.}
. This also holds for stratified simplicial sets and admissible pairings.

Proof.

Note first that
properness and admissibility are clearly sustained under restriction. As any subset of a well founded set is well founded, the same holds for regularity. Hence, the only thing to verify is that
T
restricts to a bijection. The condition on
T
guarantees that the restriction of
T
to
B_{II}\cap B_{1,n.d.}\to B_{I}\cap B_{1,n.d.}
is well defined. Injectivity is automatic, and surjectivity follows from the fact that for
\sigma\in B_{I}
,
T^{-1}\sigma
is always a face of
\sigma
. In particular, if
\sigma\in B_{1,I}
, then
T^{-1}(\sigma)\subset\sigma\subset B_{1}
. Finally, the restriction of
T
on
B_{1,\mathrm{n.d.}}
gives a proper regular pairing. This also implies that the restriction of
T
on
B_{2}\setminus B_{1}
is a proper regular pairing.
∎

We are left with showing, that
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(K)
is an SAE for
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
. We will do so in two steps. We will first construct some sub-object of
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)
,
\widehat{J}
, such that
\widehat{J}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(K)
is an SAE, following Moss’s proof in
[
Mos19
]
, and then use
Lemma

3.10
to show that it restricts to the desired SAE. To describe
\widehat{J}
and the pairing, we need to introduce some maps on subdivisions.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
n\geq 0
, and
0\leq k\leq n
. Define maps
\widetilde{j}^{k}_{n}\colon\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n})\to\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n})
and
\widetilde{r}^{k}_{n}\colon\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n+1})\to\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n})
as follows. On vertices of the form
\{i\}
, they are defined as

\widetilde{j}^{k}_{n}(\{i\})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\{i,\dots,n\}&\text{ if $i<k$}\\
\{i\}&\text{ if $i\geq k$}\end{array}\right.\text{ and }\widetilde{r}^{k}_{n}(\{i\})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\{i\}&\text{ if $i<k$}\\
\{k,\dots,n\}&\text{ if $i=k$}\\
\{i-1\}&\text{ if $i>k$}\end{array}\right.

On vertices
\sigma
, they are defined as

\widetilde{j}^{k}_{n}(\sigma)=\bigcup\limits_{i\in\sigma}\widetilde{j}^{k}_{n}(\{i\})\text{ and }\widetilde{r}^{k}_{n}(\sigma)=\bigcup\limits_{i\in\sigma}\widetilde{r}^{k}_{n}(\{i\})

And then, they are extended linearly to maps of simplicial sets. Then, given a flag
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
, the product maps

\displaystyle\widetilde{j}^{k}_{n}\times 1\colon\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n})\times N(P)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n})\times N(P)

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}^{k}_{n}\times 1\colon\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n+1})\times N(P)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n})\times N(P)

Restrict to stratum preserving maps

\displaystyle j^{k}_{n}\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}});

\displaystyle r^{k}_{n}\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{k}})

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}),

where
\mathcal{J}^{k}
is the flag obtained from
\mathcal{J}
by repeating
p_{k}
.

For
{K}
some fixed stratified simplicial set,
n\geq 0
, and
0\leq k\leq n
, let
J^{k}_{n}
be the subset of
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(K)_{n}
defined as follows

J^{k}_{n}=\{\sigma\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})\to{K}\ |\ \sigma\circ j^{k}_{n}=\sigma\}

The maps
j^{k}_{n}
and
r^{k}_{n}
satisfy a lot of relations, somewhat similar to the simplicial relations.

Proposition 3.12
.

The morphisms
j^{k},r^{k}
fulfill the following equations (for any fixed flag
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
as the target of all the compositions)

(1’)

\displaystyle r^{k}\circ\mathrm{sd}_{P}(d^{k})

\displaystyle=1_{\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}}

\displaystyle{0\leq k\leq n}

(2’)

\displaystyle j^{k}\circ r^{k}\circ\mathrm{sd}_{P}(d^{i})\circ j^{k+1}

\displaystyle=j^{k}\circ r^{k}\circ\mathrm{sd}_{P}(d^{i})

\displaystyle{0\leq k<i\leq n}

(3’)

\displaystyle r^{k}\circ\mathrm{sd}_{P}(d^{i})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sd}_{P}(d^{i})\circ r^{k-1}

\displaystyle{0\leq i<k\leq n}

(4’)

\displaystyle r^{k}\circ j^{h+1}

\displaystyle=j^{h}\circ r^{k}

\displaystyle{0\leq h\leq k\leq n}

(5’)

\displaystyle j^{k}\circ\mathrm{sd}_{P}(d^{i})\circ j^{k-1}

\displaystyle=j^{k}\circ\mathrm{sd}_{P}(d^{i})

\displaystyle{1\leq k\leq n,\text{ }0\leq i\leq n}

(6’)

\displaystyle j^{h}\circ r^{k}

\displaystyle=j^{h}\circ\mathrm{sd}_{P}(s^{k})

\displaystyle{1\leq k<h\leq n}

(7’)

\displaystyle j^{k}\circ r^{k}\circ r^{k+1}

\displaystyle=j^{k}\circ r^{k}\circ\mathrm{sd}_{P}(s^{k})

\displaystyle{0\leq k\leq n}

(8’)

\displaystyle\mathrm{sd}_{P}(s^{h})\circ j^{k}\circ r^{k}

\displaystyle=j^{k}\circ r^{k}\circ\mathrm{sd}_{P}(s^{h+1})

\displaystyle{0\leq k\leq h\leq n}

(9’)

\displaystyle\mathrm{sd}_{P}(s^{h})\circ j^{k+1}\circ r^{k+1}

\displaystyle=j^{k}\circ r^{k}\circ\mathrm{sd}_{P}(s^{h})

\displaystyle{0\leq h\leq k\leq n}

Proof.

The maps
j^{k}_{n}
and
r^{k}_{n}
are obtained from the corresponding maps in
[
Mos19
]
by conjugating them with the automorphisms
D_{n}\colon\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n})\to\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n})
sending
\{i\}
to
\{n-i\}
.
Equation
(1^{\prime})
through
(9^{\prime})
are then obtained by conjugating Moss’ equations
(1)
through
(9)

[
Mos19
, Lemma 26]
.
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

The subset
\widehat{J}\subset\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(K)
, defined as

\widehat{J}_{n}=J^{n}_{n}

is a simplicial subset. Furthermore,
\widehat{J}\subset\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)
.

Proof.

For the first part, it suffices to show that if
\sigma\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})\to{K}
is in
J^{n}_{n}
, then for all
0\leq k\leq n
, we have both

\sigma\circ\mathrm{sd}(d^{k})\in J^{n-1}_{n-1}\text{ and }\sigma\circ\mathrm{sd}(s^{k})\in J^{n+1}_{n+1}.

The former comes from equality (
5’
), while the later follows from the following equality:

(10’)

j^{n}_{n}\circ\mathrm{sd}_{P}(s^{k})\circ j^{n+1}_{n+1}=j^{n}_{n}\circ\mathrm{sd}_{P}(s^{k})

Given the definitions of the
j^{k}
, it is enough to check that
Eq.

10’
holds on vertices of
\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n+1})
of the form
\{i\}
, for the
\widetilde{j}
and
\mathrm{sd}(s^{k})
. But evaluating the transformed equation on
\{i\}
gives on both sides either
\{i,\dots,n\}
, if
k\geq i
, or
\{i-1,\dots,n\}
if
k<i
, which concludes the first part of the proof.

For the second part, Let
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
, and consider the factorization problem

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\mathcal{J}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

One checks that the map
f
, defined on vertices as
f(\sigma)=(\widetilde{j}^{n}_{n}(\sigma),\max\{p_{i}\ |\ i\in\sigma\})
, makes the diagram commute. But then, any simplex
\sigma\in\widehat{J}
must satisfy
\sigma=\sigma\circ j^{n}=\sigma\circ f\circ t_{\mathcal{J}}
. In particular, such a simplex is in
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.14
.

The inclusion
\widehat{J}\to\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(K)
is an SAE.

Proof.

Moss’ proof of
[
Mos19
, Theorem 22]
directly translates into a proof that the inclusion of
Lemma

3.14
is an SAE. Note that due to the conjugation with
D_{n}
, the inclusion relations between the
J^{k}_{n}
are reversed from those in
[
Mos19
]
. Additionally, the key difference is that in our setting,
\widehat{J}
does not coincide with
{K}
. Nevertheless,
[
Mos19
, Lemmas 27, 28 and 29]
generalize in our setting since they are direct consequences of the equalities of
[
Mos19
, Lemma 26]
, which also hold in our context in the form of
Proposition

3.12
.

To see that the pairing from
[
Mos19
]
is admissible, let
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
be some flag, and
\sigma\colon\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})\to{K}
a type
II
simplex. Then, there is some
k\geq 0
such that
T(\sigma)=\sigma\circ r^{k}
, and
\sigma=d_{k}T(\sigma)
. In particular,
T(\sigma)
is of the form
\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{k}})\to{K}
, with
\mathcal{J}^{k}
obtained from
\mathcal{J}
by repeating
p_{k}
. This means that
\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}^{k}}_{k}\to\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{k}}
is an admissible horn inclusion, and so the pairing is admissible.
∎

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
{K}\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
be a stratified simplicial set, the map
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)\to\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(K)
is an SAE.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.10
, it is enough to show that the pairing given in the proof of
Lemma

3.14
correctly restricts to
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)
. Let
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
be a flag, and
\sigma\colon\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})\to{K}
a type II simplex in
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)
. Its image under the pairing
T
is the simplex of
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(K)
,
\sigma\circ t_{\mathcal{J}}\circ r^{k}
, for some
0\leq k\leq n
, and we need to find some
\tau\in\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)
such that
\sigma\circ t_{\mathcal{J}}\circ r^{k}=\tau\circ t_{\mathcal{J}^{k}}
, where
\mathcal{J}^{k}
is obtained from
\mathcal{J}
by repeating
p_{k}
. Consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{k}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{k}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\mathcal{J}^{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\mathcal{J}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

It suffices to find a stratum preserving map
g\colon\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{k}})\to\mathrm{sd}^{\text{naiv}}_{P}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})
making the diagram commute, since then
\tau=\sigma\circ g
would satisfy
\sigma\circ t_{\mathcal{J}}\circ r^{k}=\tau\circ t_{\mathcal{J}^{k}}
. We define
g
on vertices as follows.

g(\mu)=\{i\in\widetilde{r^{k}_{n}}(\mu)\ |\ p_{i}\leq\max\{p_{j}\ |\ j\in\mu\}\}

for
\mu
a vertex of
\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{n+1})
. An elementary computation now gives commutativity.
∎

Proposition

3.9
and
Proposition

3.15
together complete the proof of
Proposition

3.1
. Indeed, we have proven that the map
K\to\mathrm{Ex}_{P}^{\text{naiv}}(K)\to\mathrm{Ex}_{P}(X)
is the composition of two SAE, hence it is a SAE.

3.2.
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
characterizes weak equivalences

Proposition

3.1
has the following immediate consequence.

Theorem 3.16
.

Let
f\colon{X}\to{Y}
be a map in
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
. It is a weak equivalence if and only if
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(f)\colon\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)\to\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(Y)
is a weak equivalence in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
. The analgous result holds for
\mathrm{Sing}_{N(P)}
and maps in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}

Proof.

By
[
Dou21a
]
we know that
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}\mathrm{Sing}_{P}
is the right functor of a Quillen-equivalence. In particular, if
f\colon{X}\to{Y}
is a map in
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
, since all objects of
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
are fibrant, it is a weak equivalence if and only if
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(f)\colon\mathrm{Ex}_{P}\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)\to\mathrm{Ex}_{P}\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(Y)
is a weak equivalence in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
. Now consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ex}_{P}\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ex}_{P}\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(Y)}

By
Proposition

3.1
, the vertical maps are trivial cofibrations and in particular weak equivalences. By two out of three, this implies that
\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(f)
is a weak equivalence if and only if
\mathrm{Ex}_{P}\mathrm{Sing}_{P}(f)
is a weak equivalence if and only if
f
is a weak equivalence. The proof for
\mathrm{Sing}_{N(P)}
is identical.
∎

4.
Realizations characterize weak-equivalences

In this section, we prove that a stratum preserving simplicial map
f:K\to L
in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
is a weak equivalence if and only if
{}{}|f|_{P}
is a weak equivalence in
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
. In light of the results in
[
Dou21a
]
which establishes that
{}{}|\mathrm{sd}_{P}{(-)}|_{P}
is the left functor in a Quillen equivalence, and those in
[
Dou21c
]
which imply that
\mathrm{sd}_{P}(K)\to K
is a weak-equivalence in
\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
, the main part is to show that
{}{}|-|_{P}
does preserve weak equivalences.
We show this result by obtaining all but the first of the weak equivalences in Diagram (
3
).

4.1.
Comparing the simplicial links and the geometrical links

We start with a comparison between the simplicial link,
{}|\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(-)|
, and the geometrical link,
{}|-|_{b}
, see
Definitions

2.34
and
2.36
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
be a stratified simplicial set and
A\subset K
a stratified simplicial subset.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be a regular flag in
P
, and
b\in{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
the barycenter. Then, there is a commutative diagram in
\mathrm{Top}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(A)|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|A|_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|K|_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the vertical maps are homeomorphisms.

The proof of the proposition relies on the following lemma, which is a direct consequence of
[
WJR13
, Theorem 2.3.2]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
h_{P}\colon{}|\mathrm{sd}(N(P))|\to{}|N(P)|
be the usual homeomorphism between a simplicial complex and its barycentric subdivision.
Let
(K,\varphi_{K})\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
be a stratified simplicial set. There exists a homeomorphism
h_{K}\colon{}|\mathrm{sd}(K)|\to{}|K|
, which induces an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
:

h_{K}\colon({}|\mathrm{sd}(K)|,h_{P}\circ{}|\mathrm{sd}(\varphi_{K})|)\to{}|(K,\varphi_{K})|_{N(P)}

Furthermore, the homeomorphism
h_{K}
restricts to homeomorphisms on all non-degenerate simplices. In particular, if
(A,\varphi_{A})\subset(K,\varphi_{K})
is a stratified simplicial subset, then the restriction of
h_{K}
induces an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}

(h_{K})_{|A}\colon({}|\mathrm{sd}(A)|,h_{P}\circ{}|\mathrm{sd}(\varphi_{A})|)\to{}|(A,\varphi_{A})|_{N(P)}

Proof.

Consider the simplicial map
\varphi_{K}\colon K\to N(P)
. Its codomain is a non-singular simplicial set, and so by
[
WJR13
, Theorem 2.3.2]
, there exist a homeomorphism
h_{K}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\mathrm{sd}(K)|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|K|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\mathrm{sd}(N(P))|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|N(P)|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\mathrm{sd}(\varphi_{K})|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\varphi_{K}|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{P}}

The commutativity of the diagram implies that
h_{K}
is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
, which is the first part of the lemma. The second part of the lemma is the content of
[
WJR13
, Proposition 2.3.23]
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.1
.

Let
A\subset K
be an inclusion of stratified simplicial sets. Since the realization functor
{}|-|\colon\mathrm{sSet}\to\mathrm{Top}
preserve pullbacks,
{}|\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)|
is given by the following pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\mathrm{sd}(K)|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{*\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\mathrm{sd}(N(P))|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\mathrm{sd}(\varphi_{K})|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|i_{\mathcal{I}}|}

On the other hand,
{}|K|_{b}
is defined as the following pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|K|_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|K|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{b\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|N(P)|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\varphi_{K}|}

By
Lemma

4.2
,
h_{K}
and
h_{P}
give an isomorphism between the arrows on the right hand side of both squares. On the other hand,
h_{P}({}|i_{\mathcal{I}}|(*))=b
, since
b
is the barycenter of
{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
, which means that there is an isomorphism between the pullback squares, producing the isomorphism
{}|\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)|\to{}|K|_{b}
. Since
h_{K}
correctly restricts to simplicial subsets, by
Lemma

4.2
, we obtain the left side of the square in
Proposition

4.1
as well as its commutativity.
∎

4.2.
From links to homotopy links

We begin by showing that, up to homotopy, the geometric link given by
{}|K|_{b}
agrees with
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
K
be a stratified simplicial set,
\mathcal{I}
a regular flag, and
y=(y_{0},\dots,y_{n})
a point in the interior of
{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
. The inclusion
\{y\}\hookrightarrow{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}
induces a weak-equivalence

(10)

r_{y}\colon\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})\to{}|K|_{y}.

The proof will take the remainder of the subsection. However, we begin with an immediate corollary.

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
K
and
L
be two stratified simplicial sets, and
f\colon{}|K|_{N(P)}\to{}|L|_{N(P)}
any map in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
. Then
f
is a weak-equivalence if and only if
f
induces weak-equivalences
{}|K|_{b}\to{}|L|_{b}

for all barycenters of simplices in
{}|N(P)|
.

Proof.

By definition of the model structure on
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
,
f:X\to Y
is a weak-equivalence if and only if
f
induces weak-equivalences
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(X)\to\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(Y)
, for all regular flags,
\mathcal{I}
. Thus, it is enough to show that the evaluation at
b
map

\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})\to{}|K|_{b}

is a weak equivalence.
This is the content of
Lemma

4.3
.
∎

We will actually prove that the map (
10
) is a homotopy equivalence. To define an inverse, we need a way to define a map
{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}\to{}|K|_{N(P)}
from the data of a point in
{}|K|_{b}
. This is the purpose of the next lemma.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
K
be a stratified simplicial set, and
\mathcal{I}
a regular flag. Let
{}|K|_{\mathrm{Int}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|)}
be the pre-image of
\mathrm{Int}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|)
under
{}|\varphi_{K}|_{N(P)}
and consider
{}|K|_{\mathrm{Int}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|)}\times{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}
as a strongly stratified space via the projection on the second factor.
Then there exists a map in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}

\rho\colon{}|K|_{\mathrm{Int}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|)}\times{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}\to{}|K|_{N(P)},

such that the restriction of
\rho
to the fiber-product
{}|K|_{\mathrm{Int}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|)}\times_{{}|\varphi_{K}|}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}
coincides with the projection on the first factor, and extends in this way to
{}|K|\times_{{}|\varphi_{K}|}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}
.

Proof.

Any point in
{}|K|_{\mathrm{Int}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|)}
can be described uniquely as a pair
(\sigma,(t_{0},\dots,t_{m}))
, where
\sigma\colon\Delta^{m}\to K
is a non-degenerate simplex such that
\varphi_{K}(\Delta^{m})
is some degeneracy of
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
(t_{0},\dots,t_{m})
is a point in the standard
m
-simplex (that is, a
(m+1)
-tuple satisfying
0\leq t_{i}\leq 1
, for all
i
and
\sum t_{i}=1
). We can relabel the
(m+1)
-tuple
(t_{0},\dots,t_{m})
as
(t^{0}_{0},\dots,t^{0}_{k_{0}},t^{1}_{0},\dots,t^{n}_{k_{n}})
so that the coordinates of
{}|\varphi_{K}|(\sigma,(t^{0}_{0},\dots,t^{0}_{k_{0}},t^{1}_{0},\dots,t^{n}_{k_{n}}))
are
(\sum t^{0}_{i},\sum t^{1}_{i},\dots,\sum t^{n}_{i})
. The condition that
{}|\varphi_{K}|(\sigma,(t^{0}_{0},\dots,t^{0}_{k_{0}},t^{1}_{0},\dots,t^{n}_{k_{n}}))\in\mathrm{Int}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
thus guarantees that
\sum t^{k}_{i}>0
, for all
0\leq k\leq n
. Now, given a point in
{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}
,
(q_{0},\dots,q_{n})
, let
\rho
be the continuous map:

\displaystyle\rho\colon{}|K|_{\mathrm{Int}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|)}\times{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}

\displaystyle\to{}|K|_{N(P)}

\displaystyle((\sigma,(t_{0},\dots,t_{m})),(q_{0},\dots,q_{n}))

\displaystyle=(\sigma,(\frac{q_{0}}{\sum_{i}t^{0}_{i}}t^{0}_{0},\dots,\frac{q_{0}}{\sum_{i}t^{0}_{i}}t^{0}_{k_{0}},\frac{q_{1}}{\sum_{i}t^{1}_{i}}t^{1}_{0},\dots,\frac{q_{n}}{\sum_{i}t^{n}_{i}}t^{n}_{k_{n}}))

For a point in the fibered product, one must have
\sum_{i}t^{l}_{i}=q_{l}
for all
0\leq l\leq n
, and
\rho
extends on it as:

\displaystyle{}|K|\times_{{}|\varphi_{K}|}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|

\displaystyle\to{}|K|

\displaystyle((\sigma,(t^{0}_{0},\dots,t^{0}_{k_{0}},t^{1}_{0},\dots,t^{n}_{k_{n}})),(q_{0},\dots,q_{n}))

\displaystyle\mapsto(\sigma,(t^{0}_{0},\dots,t^{0}_{k_{0}},t^{1}_{0},\dots,t^{n}_{k_{n}}))

∎

proof of
Lemma

4.3
.

Let
y=(y_{0},\dots,y_{n})
be a point in
\mathrm{Int}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|)
. We show that the evaluation at
y

r_{y}\colon\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})=\mathcal{C}^{0}_{N(P)}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)},{}|K|_{N(P)})\to{}|K|_{y}

is a homotopy equivalence. We define a section
{}|K|_{y}\to\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)
as follows

\displaystyle c_{y}\colon{}|K|_{y}

\displaystyle\to\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}&\to&{}|K|_{N(P)}\\
(q_{0},\dots,q_{n})&\mapsto&\rho(x,(q_{0},\dots,q_{n}))\end{array}\right.

By the previous lemma, one has
r_{y}\circ c_{y}=1_{{}|K|_{y}}
. To construct the homotopy in the other direction, we will use the following (non-stratified) identification:

\displaystyle{}|\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|\times[0,1]/{}|\partial\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|\times\{0\}

\displaystyle\simeq{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|

\displaystyle((a_{0},\dots,a_{n}),s)

\displaystyle\mapsto(sa_{0}+(1-s)y_{0},\dots,sa_{n}+(1-s)y_{n})

We now define the homotopy from
c_{y}\circ r_{y}
to the identity.

\displaystyle H\colon\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\to\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})

\displaystyle(f,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}&\to&{}|K|_{N(P)}\\
(\bar{a},s)&\mapsto&\rho(f(\bar{a},st),(\bar{a},s))\end{array}\right.

Note that
H
is well defined, since when
t\not=1
,
f(\bar{a},st)
is in
{}|K|_{\mathrm{Int}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|)}
, for all
(\bar{a},s)\in{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
, and for
t=1
, either
s\not=1
, and
f(\bar{a},s)\in{}|K|_{\mathrm{Int}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}})|}
, or
s=1
and
(f(\bar{a},1),(\bar{a},1))\in{}|K|\times_{{}|\varphi_{K}|}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
, and
\rho
is defined as the projection on the first factor.
∎

4.3.
{}|-|_{N(P)}
preserves weak equivalences

Next, we use
Corollary

4.4
to derive the following result.

Proposition 4.6
.

The functor

{}|-|_{N(P)}:\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\to\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}

preserves weak equivalences.

Proof.

Note first, that by Ken Brown’s Lemma, it suffices to show, that
{}|-|_{N(P)}
sends trivial cofibrations into weak equivalences. Now assume that
f\colon A\hookrightarrow K
is a trivial cofibration. By
Corollary

4.4
it is enough to show that for any
b\in{}|N(P)|
, the barycenter of some simplex
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, the inclusion
{}|A|_{b}\to{}|K|_{b}
is a weak-equivalence. By
Proposition

4.1
this is equivalent to showing that the inclusion
{}|\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(A)|\hookrightarrow{}|\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)|
is a weak-equivalence. But since the functor
{}|-|
preserves weak-equivalences, it is enough to show that
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(A)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(K)
is a weak-equivalence, which follows from
Lemma

4.7
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

The functor
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{}:\mathrm{sSet}_{P}\to\mathrm{sSet}
preserve trivial cofibrations.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove that
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}
sends strong anodyne extensions to strong anodyne extensions (see
Section

3
) because in both model structures, trivial cofibrations are given by retracts of strong anodyne extensions.
The functor

\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{}
is constructed by first applying a right adjoint (subdivision) and then pulling back to a vertex of
\mathrm{sd}(N(P))
. In particular, it is given by the composition of two functors preserving colimits and therefore
preserves all colimits itself. Thus, it suffices to show that
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{}
sends admissible horn inclusions into (strong) anodyne extensions in
\mathrm{sSet}
.
Further, if we show the statement for admissible horn inclusions up to a certain dimension
n
, then it automatically follows that
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}
sends all stratified strong anodyne extensions
A\to B
with
B
of dimension lesser or equal to
n
into strong anodyne extensions.

We proceed via induction over
n
.
The case
n=0
is trivial. So let
\mathcal{J}
be a flag in
P
of length
n+1
and
k\leq n+1
such that
\Lambda_{k}^{\mathcal{J}}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
is an admissible horn inclusion.
We show that
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k})}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
is a strong anodyne extension.
Let
\tau\colon\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\to\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
be the maximal non-degenerate simplex of
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
and
\tau_{k}\colon\Delta^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
be its
k
-th face.
If
\mathcal{J}
does not degenerate from
\mathcal{I}
, then by construction neither does
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
, since
\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}
is admissible. In particular,
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
does not contain the vertices corresponding to
\tau
and
\tau_{k}
.
These are precisely the vertices of
\mathrm{sd}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})
that are missing in
\mathrm{sd}(\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k})
. Hence,
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
is a bijection and there is nothing to show.

Now, if
\mathcal{J}
degenerates from
\mathcal{I}
, then so does
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
and hence both
\tau_{k}
and
\tau
define vertices in
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
.
Let
D
be the full subcomplex of
\mathrm{sd}{(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
spanned by all vertices, but the one corresponding to
\tau_{k}
, and let
D_{\mathcal{I}}
be its intersection with
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
.
We obtain a pairing on the inclusion
A:=D_{\mathcal{I}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}=:B
by taking
B_{I}
to be the set of non-degenerate simplices in
B_{n.d.}\setminus A_{n.d.}
, of the form
[\sigma_{0},...,\sigma_{m-1},\tau_{k},\tau]
, and
B_{II}
the set of simplices of the form
[\sigma_{0},\dots,\sigma_{m-1},\tau_{k}]
, for
m\geq 0
.
Then, the map sending
[\sigma_{0},...,\sigma_{m-1},\tau_{k}]
to
[\sigma_{0},...,\sigma_{m-1},\tau_{k},\tau]
is a proper regular pairing
B_{II}\to B_{I}
. Thus,
D_{\mathcal{I}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
is a SAE.
Now,
D_{\mathcal{I}}
is a cone on
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k})}
with conepoint
\tau
.
Hence by a simplicial set version of
[
Whi39
, Corollary p. 249]

it is enough to find a strong anodyne extension
\Delta^{0}\to\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k})}

to conclude that
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Lambda_{k}^{\mathcal{J}})}\hookrightarrow D_{\mathcal{I}}

is also a strong anodyne extension. Note that since
\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}
is an admissible horn and
\mathcal{J}
degenerates from
\mathcal{I}
, any stratum-preserving inclusion
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\hookrightarrow\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k}

is a strong anodyne extension. Passing to the links, we get a map
\Delta^{0}=\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}})}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}{(\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k})}
, which is a strong anodyne extension by the inductive hypothesis.
Finally, we have found two strong anodyne extensions
\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(\Lambda^{\mathcal{J}}_{k})\to D_{\mathcal{I}}
and
D_{\mathcal{I}}\to\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})
, which compose to give the desired strong anodyne extension.
∎

4.4.
Comparing homotopy links in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
and
\mathrm{Top}_{P}

In this section, we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
K
be a stratified simplicial set, and
\mathcal{I}
be some regular flag. The natural map

\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})

is a weak-equivalence.

Given that the proof of the above theorem is fairly long and somewhat involved, we give a brief summary here. First, we show
Lemmas

4.9
and
4.10
, allowing us to make two simplifying assumptions: that
\mathcal{I}=P
, and that
K
is locally finite. Then, we show (in
Lemma

4.12
) that up to homotopy, the map
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})
factors through
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})
, where
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\subset{}{}|K|_{P}
is a subspace obtained by taking away some of the least singular simplices of
K
(see
Definitions

4.11
and
6
). We then prove that the map
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})
is an homotopy equivalence (
Lemma

4.12
). This simplifies the problem because now we need to compare two homotopy links in
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
,
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})
and
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})
. To compare these two, we will decompose the inclusion
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\subset{}{}|K|_{P}
into
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},k}\subset\dots\subset{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},0}={}{}|K|_{P}
, see
Definition

4.15
, and prove that each of those induces a homotopy equivalence between homotopy links. Intuitively, homotopy inverses are obtained by sending a map
f\colon{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}\to{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l}
to some homotopic map
g\colon{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}\to{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l+1}\subset{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l}
, where the homotopy between
f
and
g
comes from precomposing
f
by some homotopy
H\colon{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}\times[0,1]\to{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}
. This homotopy should stratifiedly homotope the identity on
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}
into a smaller space, which
f
maps into
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l+1}
. As one might expect there exists no single homotopy
H
that works for arbitrary
f\in\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l})
. Instead, we first define some homotopy
S^{l}\colon{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}\times[0,1]\to{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}
(
Definition

4.17
), as well as a parameter
\alpha\colon\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l})\times[0,1]\to[0,1]
(
Lemma

4.20
). For any given
f
, the homotopy we are looking for is then obtained by combining the homotopy
S^{l}
and the parameter
\alpha
. Note that a partition of unity in
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l})
appears in the construction of
\alpha
. Such a partition of unity exists thanks to the assumption that
K
is locally finite, see
Remark

2.14
.

Before moving on to the proof, we will need the following simplifying assumptions:

As.(1)

K
is a locally finite simplicial set. This ensures that

\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})=\textbf{C}^{0}_{P}({}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P},{}{}|K|_{P})

is metrizable (when equipped with the compact open topology, see
Remark

2.14
).
Lemma

4.9
implies that we can assume that
K
is locally finite without loss of generality.

As.(2)

P=\mathcal{I}
.
Lemma

4.10
implies that we can assume this is true without loss of generality.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
K
be a stratified simplicial set, and let
A
be the category of finite stratified simplicial subsets,
K^{\alpha}\subset K
, and inclusions. Then, for all
n\geq 0
and all pointings, the following natural maps are isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\to\\
\alpha\in A\end{subarray}}\pi_{n}(\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K^{\alpha}|_{N(P)}),*)

\displaystyle\to\pi_{n}(\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)}),*),

\displaystyle\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\to\\
\alpha\in A\end{subarray}}\pi_{n}(\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K^{\alpha}|_{P}),*)

\displaystyle\to\pi_{n}(\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}),*).

Proof.

Through adjunction, one sees that an element in the homotopy group
\pi_{n}(\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)}),x)
is nothing more than a map
S^{n}\times{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}\to{}|K|_{N(P)}
, in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
, sending
*\times{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}
to
x
. In particular, such a map only reaches a finite simplicial subset of
K
, which implies the surjectivity of the map under study. The same observation for homotopies implies the injectivity.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
K
be a stratified simplicial set. Let
\widetilde{K}
be the simplicial subset defined as the following pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(P)\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{K}}

The inclusion
\widetilde{K}\to K
induces weak-equivalences

\displaystyle\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|\widetilde{K}|_{N(P)})

\displaystyle\to\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|\widetilde{K}|_{P})

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}).

Proof.

The first map is even an isomorphism. Indeed, Let
f\colon{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}\to{}|K|_{N(P)}
be a map in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
. Since geometric realization preserves pullbacks, such a map must factor through
{}|\widetilde{K}|_{N(P)}
.

For the second map, consider the following subspace of
{}{}|K|_{P}
:

Z=\{x\in{}{}|K|_{P}\ |\ \varphi_{P}\circ{}|\varphi_{K}|(x)\in\mathcal{I}\}

By construction, one has
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(Z)\cong\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})
, so it is enough to show that the inclusion
\iota\colon{}{}|\widetilde{K}|_{P}\to Z
is a stratified homotopy equivalence. Now, recall that the points of
{}{}|K|_{P}
can be described by pairs
(\sigma\colon\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\to K,\xi=(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n}))
, where
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
is some flag. With those notations, one can give an alternative description of
Z
as

Z=\{(\sigma,\xi)\in{}{}|K|_{P}\ |\ p_{m_{\xi}}\in\mathcal{I}\ \},

where
m_{\xi}=\max\{i\ |\ \xi_{i}\not=0\}
.
Given a point
(\sigma,\xi)
in
Z
, let
\xi_{\mathcal{I}}=\sum_{p_{i}\in\mathcal{I}}\xi_{i}
, and let
\epsilon_{i}
be
0
if
p_{i}\not\in\mathcal{I}
and
1
if
p_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
. With this notation, we can define a stratified retract,
r\colon Z\to\widetilde{K}
, at the level of simplices :

\displaystyle r_{\sigma}\colon Z\cap\sigma(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})

\displaystyle\to{}|\widetilde{K}|\cap\sigma(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})

\displaystyle(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n})

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{\xi_{\mathcal{I}}}(\epsilon_{0}\xi_{0},\dots,\epsilon_{n}\xi_{n})

One checks easily that those maps are compatible with faces and degeneracies, and produce a global retract
r\colon Z\to\widetilde{K}
, satisfying
r\circ i=1_{\widetilde{K}}
. Furthermore, if
(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n})\in Z\cap\sigma(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})
, then
p_{m_{\xi}}\in\mathcal{I}
and
\epsilon_{m_{\xi}}=1
. This implies that

p_{m_{\xi}}=\varphi_{P}\circ{}|\varphi_{K}|(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n})=\varphi_{P}\circ{}|\varphi_{K}|(r(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n}))=p_{m_{\xi}},

and so
r
is a stratified map. Finally, the straight-line homotopies between
\iota_{\sigma}\circ r_{\sigma}
and
1_{Z\cap\sigma(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})}
assemble to produce a stratified homotopy between
\iota\circ r
and
1_{Z}

∎

In the remainder of the subsection, we use the following notations:
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
is a flag, and
\mathcal{I}=[q_{0}<\dots<q_{k}]
is the regular flag such that
\mathcal{I}=\{p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}\}
. In other words,
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
is degenerated from
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
. Furthermore, if
p\in P
, let
\mathcal{J}_{p}=\{i\ |\ 0\leq i\leq n,\ p_{i}=p\}
. Then, for a point
(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n})\in{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|
, let
\xi_{p}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}_{p}}\xi_{i}
. Note that by construction, the stratification
{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|\to{}|N(P)|
is given by the map
(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n})\mapsto(\xi_{q_{0}},\dots,\xi_{q_{k}})
.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
K\in\mathrm{sSet}_{P}
be a stratified simplicial set. We define the subspace
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\subset{}{}|K|_{P}
as follows:

{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}=\{(\sigma,\xi)\ |\ \xi_{p}=0\Rightarrow\xi_{p^{\prime}}=0\ \forall p^{\prime}\geq p\}

Equivalently,
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}
is defined as the following pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|K|_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|N(P)|_{P}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\,.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}|\varphi_{K}|}

Figure 6.
The stratified space
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}
and the subspace
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red}}
, with
\mathcal{I}=[p_{0}<p_{1}<p_{2}]
. The dashed blue lines and the green circle indicate the missing faces.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
K
be a stratified simplicial set. There exists a homotopy equivalence,
\lambda^{*}\colon\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})
making the following diagram commute up to homotopy:

(11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{*}}

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}=[q_{0}<\dots<q_{k}]
be a regular flag, and consider the linear map
\lambda\colon{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|\to{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
sending the vertex
q_{i}
to the barycenter of
\Delta^{[q_{0}<\dots<q_{i}]}\subset\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
. We will show that the map

\displaystyle\lambda^{*}\colon\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\Big\{x\mapsto(f\circ\lambda)(x)\Big\}

has the desired properties. First note that the map
\lambda\colon{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}\to{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}
is a stratum preserving map, which means that for any
f\in\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})
,
f\circ\lambda\in\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})
. Furthermore, by construction,
\lambda({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|)\subset{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|^{\text{red}}
. In particular,
\lambda^{*}
takes values in
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})
, and it is well-defined.

Now, let
H\colon{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}\times[0,1]\to{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}
be the straight-line homotopy between
\lambda
and
1_{{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|}
. Then the map

\displaystyle H^{*}\colon\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})

\displaystyle(f,s)

\displaystyle\mapsto\Big\{\xi\mapsto f(H(\xi,s))\Big\}

gives the homotopy between the two paths in Diagram (
11
).

It remains to be shown that
\lambda^{*}
is a homotopy equivalence. Consider the map
\rho\colon{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|\to{}|K|_{N(P)}
from
Lemma

4.13
. Using the natural isomorphism
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})\simeq\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|)
,
\rho
induces a map

\rho_{*}\colon\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})\cong\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|)\to\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})

We will show that
\rho_{*}
is a homotopy inverse to
\lambda^{*}
. We first consider the composition
\rho_{*}\circ\lambda^{*}
. Consider the homotopy
H^{*}
defined above. For
f\in\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})
,
H^{*}(f,s)
lands in
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})\cong\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|)
if
s<1
and lands in
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})
for
s=1
. Let
\widehat{{}|K|}
be the strongly stratified space defined as the following union:

\widehat{{}|K|}={}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|\cup{}|K|_{N(P)}\times_{{}|N(P)|}{}|N(P)|\subset{}|K|\times_{P}{}|N(P)|\subset{}|K|\times{}|N(P)|

where the stratification is given by projecting on the second factor. The homotopy
H^{*}
factors through a map

H^{*}\colon\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}|K|_{N(P)})\times[0,1]\to\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}(\widehat{{}|K|}).

By
Lemma

4.13
,
\rho
extends to
\widehat{{}|K|}
as the identity on
{}|K|_{N(P)}
. In particular, the composition
\rho_{*}\circ H^{*}
gives a homotopy between
\rho_{*}\circ\lambda^{*}
and
1
.

For the other composition,
\lambda^{*}\circ\rho_{*}
, consider the homotopy in
\mathrm{Top}_{P}
, from
Lemma

4.13

R\colon\left({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|\right)\times[0,1]\to{}{}|K|_{P}.

Given a map in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
,
f\colon{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{N(P)}\to{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|
, and
s\in[0,1]
, consider the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|K|_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{s}}

If
s>0
,
R_{s}
takes value in
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}
. On the other hand, if
s=0
,
R_{0}=\rho
,
H_{0}=\lambda
, and
f\circ\lambda
takes value in
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red}}
. By
Remark

4.14
, this implies that
R_{0}\circ f\circ H_{0}
takes value in
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}
. Now consider the following homotopy:

\displaystyle G\colon\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|)\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})

\displaystyle(f,s)

\displaystyle\mapsto R_{s}\circ f\circ H_{s}

At
s=1
,
H_{1}=1
, and
R_{1}
is the projection to
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}
. In particular,
G_{1}
is the natural homeomorphism
\textit{H\!\hskip 1.0pto}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|)\simeq\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})
. At
s=0
,
G_{0}
is
\lambda^{*}\circ\rho_{*}
, precomposed with the aforementioned natural homeomorphism. In particular,
\lambda^{*}
is a homotopy equivalence.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

There exists a map in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}

\rho\colon{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|\to{}|K|_{N(P)},

which extends continuously to
{}|K|_{N(P)}\times_{{}|N(P)|}{}|N(P)|
as
1_{{}|K|}
, where the stratification on the domain is given by the projection on the second factor.
Furthermore, there exists a homotopy in
\mathrm{Top}_{P}

R\colon\left({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|\right)\times[0,1]\to{}{}|K|_{P}

between
\rho
and the projection to
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}
, such that for all
s>0
,
R_{s}
takes value in
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}
.

Remark 4.14
.

Note that, since the map
\rho\colon{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|\to{}|K|_{N(P)}
from
Lemma

4.13
is a strongly stratified map, its restriction to
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red}}
must take value in
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}={}|\varphi_{K}|^{-1}({}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red}})
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.13
.

Recall that if
(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n})\in{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|
, with
\mathcal{J}=[p_{0}\leq\dots\leq p_{n}]
, and
p\in P
, then
\xi_{p}=\sum_{p_{i}=p}\xi_{i}
. Now, write
\mathcal{I}=[q_{0}<\dots<q_{k}]
. By
As.(2)
,
P=\mathcal{I}
, which means that for all
0\leq i\leq n
there exists
0\leq j\leq k
such that
p_{i}=q_{j}
. Furthermore, we will label the points in
{}|N(P)|={}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
as
(t_{q_{0}},\dots,t_{q_{k}})
. We will construct
\rho
on simplices, let
\sigma\colon\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\to K
be a stratified simplex. Define
\rho_{\mathcal{J}}
as :

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathcal{J}}\colon{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|

\displaystyle\to{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{N(P)}

\displaystyle\big((\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n}),(t_{q_{0}},\dots,t_{q_{k}})\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\alpha_{0}\xi_{0},\dots,\alpha_{n}\xi_{n})

where
\alpha_{i}=\frac{t_{p_{i}}}{\xi_{p_{i}}}
, if
\xi_{p_{i}}\not=0
, and
\alpha_{i}=0
if
\xi_{p_{i}}=0
. Now, if
\xi_{q}=0
for some
q\in P
, since
(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n})
is in
{}{}|\sigma(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}})|_{P}^{\text{red}}
, this implies that
\xi_{q^{\prime}}=0
, for
q^{\prime}\geq q
. In turn,
\varphi_{P}(t_{q_{0}},\dots,t_{q_{k}})=\varphi_{P}\circ{}|\varphi_{K}|(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n})<q
. In particular, in this case, one must have
t_{q^{\prime}}=0
for
q^{\prime}\geq q
, and
\sum\alpha_{i}\xi_{i}=\sum t_{q_{j}}=1
, which implies that
\rho_{\mathcal{J}}
is well defined. If one sets
(\xi^{\prime}_{0},\dots,\xi^{\prime}_{n})=(\alpha_{0}\xi_{0},\dots,\alpha_{n}\xi_{n})
, one has
\xi^{\prime}_{q}=t_{q}
for all
q\in P
. In particular,
\rho_{\mathcal{J}}
is a map in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
. Furthermore, if we restrict
\rho_{\mathcal{J}}
to pairs
((\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n}),(t_{q_{0}},\dots,t_{q_{k}}))
such that
{}|\varphi_{K}|(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n})=(t_{q_{0}},\dots,t_{q_{k}})
, then, for all
0\leq i\leq n
, either
\xi_{p_{i}}\not=0
and
\alpha_{i}=1
, or
\xi_{p_{i}}=0
, and in both cases,
\alpha_{i}\xi_{i}=\xi_{i}
. Using this
\rho_{\mathcal{J}}
can be extended to
{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{N(P)}\times_{{}|N(P)|}{}|N(P)|
by the identity (or, more precisely, the projection on the first factor). Assembling all the
\rho_{\mathcal{J}}
together gives the desired map.

Now, to produce the homotopy
R
, consider the following straight-line homotopy defined on simplices, where the
\alpha_{i}
are defined as above:

\displaystyle R_{\mathcal{J}}\colon\left({}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}^{\text{red}}\times_{P}{}|N(P)|\right)\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\to{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}

\displaystyle((\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n}),(t_{q_{0}},\dots,t_{q_{k}}),s)

\displaystyle\mapsto((s+(1-s)\alpha_{0})\xi_{0},\dots,(s+(1-s)\alpha_{n})\xi_{n})

To see that
R_{\mathcal{J}}
is a stratum preserving map, notice that if
s=0
,
R_{\mathcal{J}}
is just
\rho_{\mathcal{J}}
which is a map in
\mathrm{Top}_{N(P)}
.
If
s>0
,
(s+(1-s)\alpha_{i})\not=0
for all
0\leq i\leq n
and so
(s+(1-s)\alpha_{i})\xi_{i}=0\Leftrightarrow\xi_{i}=0
for all
0\leq i\leq n
. This implies two things. First,
\varphi_{P}\circ{}|\varphi_{K}|(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{n})=\varphi_{P}\circ{}|\varphi_{K}|((s+(1-s)\alpha_{0})\xi_{0},\dots,(s+(1-s)\alpha_{n})\xi_{n})
, and so
R_{\mathcal{J}}
is a stratified homotopy, and second,
R_{\mathcal{J}}
takes value in
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}^{\text{red}}
for all
s>0
.
Finally, notice that for
s=1
,
R_{\mathcal{J}}
is the projection on
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}|_{P}
, and so
R_{\mathcal{J}}
is a homotopy between
\rho_{\mathcal{J}}
and this projection. Assembling the
R_{\mathcal{J}}
gives the desired homotopy.
∎

In order to prove
Theorem

4.8
, it remains to be shown that the map induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}})\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P})
is a weak-equivalence. We will do so by further decomposition.

Definition 4.15
.

Write
\mathcal{I}=[q_{0}<\dots<q_{k}]
, and let
0\leq l\leq k
. Define the following subspace of
{}{}|K|_{P}
:

{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l}=\{(\sigma,\xi)\ |\ \xi_{p}=0\Rightarrow\xi_{p^{\prime}}=0,\ \forall p^{\prime}\geq p,\ \forall p<q_{l}\}.

We then have a sequence of inclusions

{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red}}={}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},k}\subset\dots\subset{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},0}={}{}|K|_{P}.

Theorem

4.8
then follows from the following lemma.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
0\leq l\leq k-1
, the inclusion
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l+1}\to{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l}
induces a homotopy equivalence

\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l+1})\to\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l})

In order to prove
Lemma

4.16
, we will need a few more technical results.

Definition 4.17
.

Let
0\leq l\leq k-1
, define the map
S^{l}
as

\displaystyle S^{l}\colon{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\to{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|

\displaystyle((t_{q_{0}},\dots,t_{q_{k}}),s)

\displaystyle\mapsto(t_{q_{0}},\dots,t_{q_{l-1}},t_{q_{l}}+(1-s)\sum_{j=l+1}^{k}t_{q_{j}},st_{q_{l+1}},\dots,st_{q_{k}}).

Lemma 4.18
.

The map
S^{l}
is stratum preserving outside of
s=0
, and it restricts to the projection on the first factor on the subspace

{}|\Delta^{[q_{0}<\dots<q_{l}]}|=\{(t_{q_{0}},\dots,t_{q_{k}})\ |\ t_{q}=0\ \forall q>q_{l}\}.

Proof.

Let
t=(t_{q_{0}},\dots,t_{q_{k}})
, and
s\in[0,1]
, and write
t^{\prime}=S^{l}(t,s)
. If
\varphi_{P}(t)=q\leq q_{l}
, then
t_{q^{\prime}}=t^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}=0
for all
q^{\prime}>q
, and
S^{l}(t,s)=t
for all
s
, this addresses the second part of the lemma. If
\varphi_{P}(t)=q>q_{l}
, then for
s>0
, one has
t^{\prime}_{q}=st_{q}>0
, and
t^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}=0
for all
q^{\prime}>q
. In particular,
\varphi_{P}(t^{\prime})=q
, and
S^{l}(-,s)
is a stratum preserving map.
∎

Lemma 4.19
.

For all
0\leq l\leq k-1
,
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l+1}
is a neighborhood of the
q_{l}
-stratum of
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l}

Proof.

Note that the subspace
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l}
can be defined as the following pullback square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|K|_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red},l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}|\varphi_{K}|_{P}}

Now since
{}{}|\varphi_{K}|_{P}
is a continuous, stratum preserving map, it is enough to show that
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red},l+1}
is a neighborhood of the
q_{l}
-stratum of
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red},l}
. Now, a point in the
q_{l}
-stratum of
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red},l}
is of the form
(t_{q_{0}},\dots,t_{q_{k}})
, with
t_{q_{l}}\not=0
and
t_{q}=0
for all
q>q_{l}
. Furthermore, the defining condition of
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red},l}
implies that
t_{q}\not=0
for all
q<q_{l}
. This means that there exist a neighborhood of
t
,
U\subset{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
, such that for any
t^{\prime}\in U
,
q\leq q_{l}\Rightarrow t^{\prime}_{q}\not=0
. But this means that all points in
U
satisfy the defining condition of
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red},l+1}
. In particular,
U\subset{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red},l+1}
, and the latter is a neighborhood of the
q_{l}
-stratum of
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}^{\text{red},l}
.
∎

Lemma 4.20
.

There exists a continuous map
\alpha\colon\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l})\times[0,1]\to[0,1]
, such that

(1)

\alpha(f,s)=0\Rightarrow s=0
,

(2)

f(S^{l}(t,\alpha(f,\sum_{j=l+1}^{k}t_{q_{j}})))\in{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l+1}
, for all
t=(t_{q_{0}},\dots,t_{q_{k}})\in{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
and for all
f\in\mathcal{H}\!\textit{o}\mathrm{Link}_{\mathcal{I}}({}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l})
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
, and define
{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|^{\epsilon,l}
as the following subset of
{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|
:

{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|^{\epsilon,l}=\{t\in{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|\mid\sum_{i=l}^{k}t_{q_{i}}\geq\epsilon\}

Now, pick some stratified map
f\colon{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}\to{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l}
, and consider the intersection of the following nested family of compact subsets:

\bigcap_{\alpha>0}f(S^{l}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|^{\epsilon,l}\times[0,\alpha]))=f(S^{l}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|^{\epsilon,l}\times\{0\}))

Note that if
t\in{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|^{\epsilon,l}
,
S^{l}(t,0)
is in the
q_{l}
-stratum of
{}{}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|_{P}
. In particular,
f(S^{l}({}|\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}|^{\epsilon,l}\times\{0\}))
is a compact subset of the
q_{l}
-stratum of
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l}
. Since, by
Lemma

4.19
,
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l+1}
is a neighborhood of the
q_{l}
-stratum of
{}{}|K|_{P}^{\text{red},l}
, there must exist
\alpha^{\epsilon}>0
such that
